**Preface**

We live in a time when computer applications have revolutionized all enterprises and changed the substance of our society. Because of their boundless utility and expanding ubiquity, a study of computer applications and their impact on our daily life has become mandatory. Programming languages are required to develop efficient and effective computer applications. Python Programming gains significance in this context in that it is an elegant and powerful language aimed at non-professional programmers.

Python is an easy-to-use, powerful and interpreted high-level programming language for general-purpose programming. Developed under open-source license, it supports object oriented programming with classes and multiple inheritance. It also supports addition of new modules implemented in a compiled language such as C or C++. Python contains advanced programming features such as generators and list comprehensions. It can run on almost all platforms.

**ABOUT THE BOOK**

This book is designed to bring readers up to speed on Python language fundamentals and will be useful to undergraduate students of computer science and information technology as well as to those pursuing masters degree in computer applications. The objective of this book is to improve the efficiency of students in using Python to solve real-world problems.

The book begins with an introduction to computer systems and analyzes the defining features of the Python Programming language. With a thorough discourse on the basics of Python programming, it elaborates on the language’s characteristic elements before exploring ways to apply it in common application domains and realistically scaled tasks. At the end of each chapter, there are numerous Sample Programs, MCQs, Fill in the Blanks and True/False questions for the students’ benefit. In addition, there are case studies that demonstrate how decision making and looping statements, along with Python’s built-in and user-defined functions, can be adopted for practical applications.

**ORGANIZATION**

The book contains 19 chapters and four appendices. **[Chapter 1](#ch01.xhtml)** provides an introduction to computer systems and covers the concept of generations of computers, while also providing an insight into the applications and classification of computers. It also includes computer hardware and software, giving detailed information on various levels of programming languages and problem solving approaches such as algorithms, pseudo code and flowcharts. **[Chapter 2](#ch02.xhtml)** discusses about the features and applications of Python programming language. It compares the essential elements of Python with those of C and Java programming languages and illustrates installation of the Python software, step by step. **[Chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml)** elaborates on the syntax and style used in Python programming language and describes the various operators and expressions supported by Python. **[Chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml)** explains the different types of decision making statements such as if, if.. else and nested if and analyzes the use of while loop, for loop, break statement, continue statement and pass statement in Python programs. **[Chapter 5](#ch05.xhtml)** deals with Sequences (strings, lists, tuples, byte arrays, buffers and range objects) in the Python programming language. It also spells out the properties of List Function.

**[Chapter 6](#ch06.xhtml)** focuses on the concept of tuples and their immutable properties and shows how these form efficient sequences in Python. The chapter also discusses the various operations that can be performed on a tuple object. **[Chapter 7](#ch07.xhtml)** expounds on the concept of Dictionaries and shows the different ways of creating a dictionary object. **[Chapter 8](#ch08.xhtml)** deals with functions in Python programming language. It examines defining and calling functions, recursive functions and the four types of arguments, viz., required arguments, default arguments, keyword arguments and variable length arguments. **[Chapter 9](#ch09.xhtml)** delves into the creation of a module and the various ways of importing elements from a module. **[Chapter 10](#ch10.xhtml)** exemplifies the object oriented properties of encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance and polymorphism.

**[Chapter 11](#ch11.xhtml)** elucidates the handling of packages in Python. It also covers installation of packages and importing packages. **[Chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml)** explores how strings are handled in Python. This chapter also depicts the use of regular expressions to handle text patterns efficiently. **[Chapter 13](#ch13.xhtml)** scrutinizes the various operations performed on files such as read, write, open and close. The chapter also covers serialization and de-serialization, hash files and dictionary access. **[Chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml)** discusses about the various types of errors and exceptions with simple examples. **[Chapter 15](#ch15.xhtml)** reveals how multithreading is handled in Python and deals at length with thread synchronization. **[Chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml)** delineates the use of Tkinter based GUI and asserts its use for the design of GUI based applications using various widgets such as label, button, entry and list box. **[Chapter 17](#ch17.xhtml)** is dedicated to the handling of events in Python. **[Chapter 18](#ch18.xhtml)** lays out the standard libraries available in Python. **[Chapter 19](#ch19.xhtml)**, the last chapter of the book, outlines the concept of testing in Python programming with emphasis on unit testing and loading and running tests.

The book has four useful appendices: [Appendix A](#app01.xhtml) deals with networking concepts in Python; [Appendix-B](#app02.xhtml) explains Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) for sending the mails from one account to another; [Appendix C](#app03.xhtml) deals with the installation of the plotly package for graphs while [Appendix-D](#app04.xhtml) provides basic information on Common Gateway Interface (CGI).

With adequate focus and effort, we are sure that students will find this book rewarding since it has been written to shorten their start-up time to familiarize themselves with Python and provide a solid foundation in Python’s manifold application domains.

**ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS**

At the outset, we convey our special thanks to Universities Press (India) Private Limited for giving us an opportunity to work with them. We take this moment to thank Ramesh and Kallol Das, editors, who added value to our book. We also thank the entire team that was involved in this assignment.

We are obliged to the management of our respective colleges for their support, which helped us in no small measure while we were working on the manuscript. We are also indebted to our college staff for their constant encouragement and support.

Finally, we thank our respective family members without whose love and understanding, this work may not have come to fruition.

M Radhika Mani
B N Jagadesh**

**1.1 Introduction to Computer Systems**

The computer is a collection of hardware and software components used to perform a specific operation on an existing hardware. There are several characteristics of a computer system such as its speed, accuracy, storage etc. which are used to evaluate its efficiency.

**1.1.1 Generations of computers**

The generations of computers are characterized by the hardware used in them. There are five generations of computers and in each generation, there was a major technological advancement that fundamentally changed the way computers operate.

■ The first-generation computers were designed based on vacuum tubes. It had magnetic drumbased memory and punch card-based inputs. The size of the first-generation computer was large.

■ The second-generation computers were designed using transistors. Thus, the size of the second-generation computer was less when compared with first-generation computer. It contained magnetic core-based memory.

■ The third-generation computers were designed with Integrated Circuits (ICs). The integrated circuit is smaller in size when compared with the transistor, whereas its memory capacity is much higher. Thus the size of the third generation computer is smaller when compared with the second-generation computer. It contains enhanced magnetic core-based memory.

■ During the fourth generation, the micro computer, personal computers were introduced. The size of fourth-generation computer is lesser than that of the third-generation computer.

■ The fifth generation saw the advent of multi-core computers that have now become indispensible for performing artificial intelligence, machine vision, virtual reality etc.

**1.1.2 Applications of computers**

Today, computers are used in various fields and they have become an integrated part of our life. Computers are routinely used in the areas of

■ Education

■ Business

■ Industry

■ Automation

■ Textile

■ Transportation

■ Navigation

■ Surveillance

■ Weather forecasting

■ Medicine

**1.1.3 Classification of computers**

The computers are classified into various types as listed below.

■ Micro computer

■ Laptop computer

■ Work station

■ Super computer

■ Main Frames

■ Hand held computer

■ Multi core computer

**1.2 Computer Hardware**

The computer is mainly used to perform various operations on data. The computer hardware represents the physical elements in the computer. The block diagram of a computer is shown in [Fig. 1.1](#ch01.xhtml_fig1.1).

![image](images/9789386235633_3.jpg)

**Figure 1.1**Computer Hardware

**1.2.1 Input/ Output devices**

The input devices are used to obtain data from the user. The data given by the user is transferred to the CPU. Examples of input devices are keyboard, scanner etc. The output devices are used to deliver data to the user. The data from the CPU are transferred to the output devices. Monitor, printer etc. are examples of output devices. The speed of the CPU is high and the speed of the Input/Output (I/O) devices is low. Thus, during the communication between CPU and the I/O devices, CPU becomes idle. To avoid the wastage of CPU time, the I/O processor is introduced between the CPU and I/O devices.

**1.2.2 CPU**

The Central Processing Unit (CPU) coordinates the complete computer. In the computer, CPU is responsible for performing any operation. For this, CPU contains two elements. They are the control unit and Arithmetic Logical and Shift Unit (ALU). The ALU is responsible for performing the operation on the given operands. The control unit generates control signals. With these control signals, CPU can have control over all the other components.

**1.2.3 Memory**

In addition to the execution of operation on the data, the computer is responsible for maintaining data. For this, the computer uses memory. The memory is responsible to maintain data and can be represented as M×N size. Thus, the memory contains M number of locations, with each location storing a word size of N bits. On any memory location, either read operation or write operation can be performed. There are various types of memory elements in the computer. The CPU can have a direct connection with main memory. There are two types of main memory. They are i) Random Access Memory (RAM) and ii) Read Only Memory (ROM). The RAM allows performing both read and writing operations whereas the ROM allows performing only read operation. Other than main memory, various other types of memory elements are allowed in the computer. They are cache memory, virtual memory and auxiliary memory.

**1.3 Computer Software**

Computer software is a set of programs that can be executed by a processor. With the software, the computer can able to perform a specific task. There are two types of computer software. They are

1\. System software

2\. Application software

**1.3.1 System software**

System software is a collection of programs used to perform the operation or task for the computer. In general, operating system is considered as system software. With the system software, the computer can be handled efficiently.

**1.3.2 Application software**

Application software is a collection of programs intended to perform a particular application. They are also called as applications and are end-user programs. Application software can be executed on top of the system software.

**1.4 Programming Languages**

Programming languages are used for the development of a solution to a problem. There are three types of programming languages. They are machine-level, assembly-level and high-level programming languages.

**1.4.1 Machine-level programming language**

During the early days of the computer development, programmers had developed the code only in computer readable format. The computer can understand everything in digital format only. So, the instructions were written in the form of 0 and 1\. This machine-level code is in the format which can be directly understood by the computer. The programmer had to remember the length of the complicated machine-level instructions for the development of a program. It was observed that the complexity of the machine code was high but the time required to execute the machine code was less. The programs developed by using machine-level language were system-dependent.

**1.4.2 Assembly-level programming language**

In case of machine-level programming language, the complexity is high for the programmers to develop their programs. The assembly-level language was introduced to overcome this. The assembly-level language allows the programmer to write the instructions with short codes of the operation. These short codes are also called as “pneumonics”. The assembly-level language instruction consists of the operational code (also called as “opcode”) and the operand. The operands represent the data on which the opcode should be performed. As the computer can understand only the machine code, it is necessary to convert the assembly-level language code to machine code. This conversion process is performed by the “Assembler”. When compared to the execution of machine-level language program, the assembly-level language programs required additional time for the conversion process. Hence, programmers still feel that the assembly-level language code is complex.

**1.4.3 High-level programming language**

To overcome the complexity of the assembly-level language program, the high-level programming language was introduced. High-level programming language is programmer friendly since the building blocks of this language could be easily read and understood by humans. For example, the addition operation can be represented in various languages as shown below.

Machine level: 10101000100000100… (Binary format)

Assembly level: ADD

High level: +

The “+” is used regularly. So, writing the programs with regular symbols becomes easy for the programmer. But the computer can understand only the machine code. Thus, the high-level code should be converted into machine code. For this conversion, two elements are available. They are “compiler” and “interpreter”. Both compiler and interpreter are used for converting high-level codes to machine codes. The compiler will read the entire high-level code and then start the conversion process. The interpreter will read a line of high-level code and then convert it to machine code, performing line-by-line conversion.

**1.5 Algorithmic Problem Solving**

Programming languages are used for developing programs that help in solving problems. For solving a problem, various steps are to be followed as given below.

■ Initially, the problem for which the solution has to be given has to be analyzed. With this step, the problem should be reviewed thoroughly and the developer should understand the definition of the problem. Finally, the developer should learn the inputs and outputs required for the problem and the implementation plan.

■ In the second step, the methodology for the implementation plan should be identified. Further, the implemented methodology should be decomposed into various small parts. Each small part is considered as a task.

■ In the third step, an algorithm has to be developed for the methodology and its tasks. With the help of algorithm, the developer will understand the step-by-step instruction to be performed to get the desired end output for solving the problem.

■ In the fourth step, a program is developed by following the algorithm. For this, the developer has to use any one of the programming languages.

■ Finally, the developed program should be compiled and then executed to get the solution for the given problem.

**1.5.1 Algorithms**

Algorithm is a step-by-step approach for representing the problem solving process. The algorithm can be written in any language such as English etc. It is a blue-print of the methodology which will be developed by any one of the programming language. With an algorithm, the solution for a problem can be derived in a finite number of steps. The algorithm should ensure the termination of the methodology. It should be efficiently developed. With an efficient algorithm, the developed program will have less complexity. The complexity of the algorithm can be represented in terms of time and space. Time complexity represents the time required for the completion of program execution while space complexity represents the total amount of memory space required for the completion of program execution. Space complexity includes the memory space allocated for all elements in the program during execution.

**1.5.2 Characteristics of algorithm**

■ The algorithm should be simple.

■ The algorithm should be written in a sequential order of steps. The developer should not write the (n+1)th step before the nth step of the algorithm.

■ The algorithm should consist of a finite number of steps.

■ The algorithm should start and terminate properly.

■ The algorithm should give the solution of the problem.

■ The algorithm should use have less space and time complexities.

■ The algorithm should have at least one input and output.

**1.5.3 Advantages and disadvantages of algorithm**

Algorithms have the following advantages:

■ They are simple to develop

■ They provide better understanding of the methodology.

■ Debugging is easy with sequential steps.

■ Development of program with the programming language is easy

However, an important disadvantage is that it is time-consuming for developing an algorithm and its corresponding program.

**1.5.4 Algorithm notation**

Algorithm notations are used to represent the algorithm in various formats. There are various notations used for an algorithm as listed below.

**Step form**

The step form allows the developer to write the algorithm with various statements. Each statement represents a task of the problem solution and a group of statements will give the final solution to the problem. The step form is written with the help of statements. It consists of sequenced steps for each statement.

**Example:** The step form of the algorithm the problem “Read and display the number.”

1\. Start

2\. Read the number

3\. Display the number

4\. Stop

**Pseudo code**

The written format of the algorithm is called as pseudo code. This is a limited edition of the step form. In this notation, the statements are written with limited terminology of the general language. It focuses on high-level description of each statement of the algorithm. It does not reflect on the actual programming language code but it contains the short codes for the development of the program.

**Example:** The pseudo code of the algorithm the problem “Read and display the number.”

1\. Start

2\. INPUT N

3\. PRINT N

4\. Stop

**Flowchart**

The graphical representation of an algorithm is called as flowchart. The flowchart contains various symbols and simple statements for representing each element of the algorithm. Some of them are listed below.

![image](images/9789386235633_4.jpg)

**Example:**

![image](images/9789386235633_5.jpg)

**1.5.5 Building blocks of algorithms**

The algorithm should derive an efficient solution to a problem. The various elements of an algorithm are listed below.

■ The “statement” is used to express an action or event. In an algorithm, statements should be written in sequential order. The statement can have multiple sub-statements. The statement can either be simple statement or compound statement.

■ The state of the algorithm represents its current status. The first state of the algorithm is its initial state. From the initial state, it enters into successive states and finally enters the ending state. With the final ending state, the algorithm will be terminated.

■ The control flow or flow of control is used to evaluate the statements in an order to get the result. The control flow starts with the start statement and ends with the stop statement.

■ A function is a collection of ordered sub-statements which can be grouped into a single entity. They are also called as subroutine. The function can be executed multiple times. There are three building blocks of the algorithm. They are listed below.

**Sequence structure**

The sequence structure of the algorithm is used to represent an action or event. In the sequential structure, the current action or event leads to the next ordered action or event. Skipping of action or event is not permitted in this structure.

**Example:** “Read a number and display the absolute value of the number.”

1\. Start

2\. Read the number

3\. Compute the absolute value of the number

4\. Display the absolute value of the number

5\. Stop

**Selection structure**

The selection structure of the algorithm contains the decision making statements. The outcome of the decision is a Boolean value, i.e., True or False. Based on the outcome of the decision making statement, the decision is made whether to ignore the set of statements or not. Various examples of selection structure are if... then and if... then...else.

**Example:**

“Read a number and check whether the number is positive.”

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. if N is positive then identify the number as positive number

4\. Stop

“Read a number and check whether the number is positive or negative.”

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. if N is positive then identify the number as positive number else identify the number as negative number

4\. Stop

**Iteration structure**

The iteration process of the algorithm is made up of repetition or loop statements. The iteration structure is used to repeat the statements for a finite number of times; for example, Repeat and While Statements. The repetition is performed based on the evaluation of condition.

**Example of iterative statement**: “Read an N value and print the hello message N times.”

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. Repeat

Print “hello”

Decrement N value

until N is non positive number

4\. Stop

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. While N is non positive number

4\. begin

5\. Print “hello”

6\. Decrement N value

7\. end

8\. Stop

**1.5.6 Steps for developing algorithms**

Algorithmic problem solving uses the following steps.

■ Prepare a detailed report of the problem to be solved.

■ Conduct a detailed analysis of the problem report.

■ Develop a well-defined algorithm for the analyzed problem.

■ Review the algorithm and update it with additional features, if required.

■ Test and evaluate the validity of the algorithm.

■ Develop the program for the algorithm.

**1.5.7 Simple strategies for developing algorithms**

The iterative approach of the algorithm includes repeated execution of statements until the condition is satisfied. In an algorithm, repeat, while and for are used for iteration.

**Example**: “Read an N value and print the factorial of N” by using iterative approach.

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. Initialize X with 1

4\. Repeat

5\. multiply X with N

6\. Decrement N value by 1

7\. until N is greater than 1

8\. Print the value of X

9\. Stop

The recursive approach of the algorithm includes calling function repeatedly until the condition is satisfied.

**Example**: “Read an N value and print the factorial of N” by using recursive approach.

**Algorithm 1: FACT (Positive integer K)**

1\. Start

2\. If N is 1

then return 1

else return FACT(N-1)

3\. Stop

**Algorithm 2:**

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. Store the result of FACT(N) in X

4\. Print the value of X

5\. Stop

**1.5.8 Find minimum in a list**

1\. Start

2\. Input N values and store them in list L

3\. X← L[0]

4\. I←1

5\. Repeat

6\. if X >L[I]

7\. then X←L[I]

8\. until I is less than or equal to N

9\. Print the value of X

10\. Stop

**1.5.9 Insert a card in a list of sorted cards**

1\. Start

2\. Input N sorted cards and store them in list L

3\. Input the new card C

4\. Identify the position of the card where the current position value is>C and next position value<C

5\. If position is not identified

6\. then consider the end of the list as the position

7\. Insert C into the identified position of L

8\. Print the value of X

9\. Stop

**1.5.10 Guess an integer number in a range**

1\. Start

2\. Input N

3\. Generate a random number between 1 and 100 and store it in X

4\. If X is equal to N

5\. then print “matched”

6\. else if X is less than N

7\. then print “less value”

8\. else print “greater value”

9\. Stop

**1.5.11 Towers of Hanoi algorithm**

1\. Start

2\. Function H(disk, source, destination, aux)

3\.    IF disk == 0, THEN

4\.       move disk from source to destination

5\.    ELSE

6\.       H(disk - 1, source, aux, destination)

7\.       move disk from source to destination

8\.       H(disk - 1, aux, destination, source)

9\.    END IF

10\. END Function

11\. Stop

**1.6 Building Blocks of Program**

The collection of instructions grouped into a single place called as program. A program consists of multiple instructions. Each instruction in a program is called as a statement. After developing a program for the required application, the programmer will save the program. Thus, the program will be stored into the memory. After compilation process, the corresponding machine level code will be stored in the memory. During the execution of the program, the memory will be read, statement by statement, and the processor will carry out the instructions of the corresponding statement. Various building blocks of a program are listed below.

■ Statements

■ Data types

■ Variables

■ Keywords

■ Expressions

■ Operands

■ Operators

■ Functions

■ Conditions and loops

**1.7 Background of Learning to Write Programs**

■ Learn the character set which refers to the set of alphabets, letters and some special characters that are valid in the programming language.

■ Learn the tokens that are valid in the programming language. With this, the programmer will know the valid words.

■ Then, learn the instruction format allowed in the programming language. With this, the programmer will be able to write valid sentences.

■ Finally, the programmer should be able to group valid multiple instructions into a program in the programming language.

**1.8 Sample Algorithms**

**Algorithm 1:** Read a string and check whether it is palindrome or not.

1\. Start

2\. Input String S

3\. l←length of S

4\. i←l-1

5\. while i>=0

6\. begin

7\. Store the cuurent character of S into string T

8\. Decrement i value

9\. end

10\. If S is equal to T

11\. then print “palindrome”

12\. else print “not palindrome”

13\. stop

**Algorithm 2:** Read N number of values and add N values

1\. Start

2\. Read N numbers

3\. i←0

4\. S←0

5\. while i<N

6\. begin

7\. S←S+i

8\. i←i+1

9\. end

10\. print S

11\. stop

**Algorithm 3:** Read N and print the Fibonacci series with N numbers

1\. Start

2\. Read N

3\. Fib[0]←0

4\. Fib[1]←1

5\. i←2

6\. while i<=N

7\. begin

8\. Fib[i]←Fib[i-1]+Fib[i-2]

9\. i←i+1

10\. End

11\. Print Fib

12\. Stop

**Glossary**

■ The computer program consists of a set of instructions that provide solution to a given problem.

■ An algorithm is a step-by-step approach for the development of a program.

■ A flowchart is a graphical representation of the program.

**Summary**

■ A computer program helps in solving complex problems.

■ To develop a computer program, initially an algorithm should be developed and then a programming language should be used to develop a program for the algorithm.

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Fundamentals of Python Programming** |
| **2** |   |

The present chapter discusses about the features, applications of Python programming language. It compares C, Java and Python programming languages and illustrates installation of the Python software, step by step.

**2.1 Introduction to Python**

Python is a simple and powerful language. It helps the programmer find solution to a given problem without worrying about the technical details of the programming language viz., structure and syntax. It supports high-level programming language with both procedure and object-oriented properties and is suitable for scripting and development of various applications. The python programming language is introduced by Guido van Rossum. Various popular programming languages such as C, C++ and Java can be coupled with Python. It contains built-in libraries for data structures and GUI (graphical user interface). In Python, a control routine program is an additional feature. This program feature helps Python to connect to different programs. There is also a built-in garbage collector feature, which helps Python to handle memory management efficiently. In Python, inclusion of various libraries is not required. Further, usage of code blocks is made easy with the indentation (white space).

In Python, declaration of a variable does not require the specification of the data type. So, it is called a dynamic typed language. It is applicable for derived types also. The Python is a platform-independent programming language. It contains an interpreter which will read the source program line by line and convert it into machine code. Similar to Java programming language, the Python also supports platform-independent feature with the help of Byte code. The interpreter of Python will generate the Byte code as the output. This byte code file can be used by any platform and within each platform, the Byte code is executed. The Python uses the interpreter more than the compiler; so it takes more time. The advantage of using interpreter is that the syntax errors can be identified immediately while reading a program line. The Python programming language is popular for program prototyping and is used for developing programs within a short time. It can support program modifications with the support of graphical user interfaces (GUIs).

The Python supports both procedural oriented and object oriented programming language. In a single Python program, both these programming language features can be used. So, Python programming supports the tops–down approach which allows the usage of functions. It also supports bottom–up approach which allows the usage of classes and objects.

**2.2 Features of Python**

■ **Unsophisticated**

It is an unsophisticated language. It allows the user to write the programs without following strict rules viz., declaring the variable data types, usage of curly braces etc. It allows the programmer to provide the solution program rather than focusing on the structure and syntax.

■ **Easy**

Learning of Python programming language is very easy, even for beginners. Python is a Free/Libre and Open Source Software (FLOSS). This is a software given by FLOSS community for knowledge sharing.

■ **High-level**

The programmer need not worry about various low-level details required for the program.

■ **Portable**

The Python is a platform-independent programming language. With the Byte code, the Python Program can be executed by any platform and hence, Python programs are portable.

■ **Interpreted**

Python programs can be directly executed from the Byte code. The Byte code is an executable code but it is an interpreted code. It translates the original source code into platform-independent code.

■ **Object Oriented**

Python supports all the principles of object-oriented programming (OOP). Python programs can be designed around the objects which concentrate on data. Implementing various OOP principles in Python is simple as compared to C++ or Java.

■ **Extensibility**

These programs can be executed by the Python and are extensible to C, C++ and Java. In this case, the programs can be treated as disabled codes.

■ **Embeddable**

The scripting feature can be provided by embedding the Python program into C or C++ programs.

■ **Extensive Libraries**

The Python consists of a huge standard library for handling regular expressions, threading, GUI etc.

**2.3 Applications of Python**

The Python can be useful for various real-time applications as listed below.

**2.3.1 Web and internet development**

The Python provides various features for handling the Web and the Internet. It is used for development of various frameworks (Pyramid and Django etc.), Micro frameworks (Bottle and Flask etc.) and content management systems (CMS and Plone etc.). For this, Python extensive libraries should support HTML, FTP, XML, JSON, Socket and E-mail processing. For supporting asynchronous network programming, Twisted Python framework is used.

**2.3.2 Scientific and numeric**

Python is widely used in scientific and numeric computing. For handling mathematics, engineering and science, SciPy framework is used. To analyze and model the data, Pandas framework is used. For parallel computation and visualization, IPython framework is used.

**2.3.3 Education**

For students, Python is a powerful language to learn programming skills. By learning Python programming, the student will be able to develop various real-time applications. Issues that may arise while teaching the language are discussed in the Education Special Interest Group.

**2.3.4 Desktop GUIs**

For Python, Tk GUI library provides the binary distribution. There are also platform-specific libraries such as GTK+. Kivy and wxWidgets are widely used toolkits for various platforms.

**2.3.5 Software development**

Nowadays, Python is widely used as a standard programming language for developing software. Python can be used for various applications such as building, controlling, management and testing. For building and controlling, sCons framework is used; for testing, Buildbot framework is used and for management, Trac framework is used.

**2.6 Python Virtual Machine**

The Python Virtual Machine (PVM) is used to provide platform independence while developing application software. The PVM is a part of the Python interpreter that helps to understand the pre-compiled or byte code of the source python program. The compiled byte code is available in “.pyc” file. This file contains a magic tag and a modification time stamp tag. Each tag has a length of 4 bytes. The .pyc file is simple and can be transferred. Its loading capacity is high and it provides more security. These files are not compatible between various versions of Python.

**2.7 Frozen Binaries**

For any Python program, the frozen binary file can be created with various components such as the byte code of the source program, interpreter or PVM and the corresponding supported files. All these files are packaged into a single binary file which is executable. “py2exe”, “PyInstaller” and “freeze” are used for creating frozen binaries.

**2.8 Memory Management in Python**

In Python, the memory is considered as private heap. All the Python objects are created by using private heap only. The Python memory manager is responsible for allocating and deallocating the memory for the objects. The raw memory allocator will interact directly with the operating system and can manage all Python data. The object-specific allocators are used to manage the specific object data only.

■ PyMem_RawMalloc(), PyMem_RawCalloc(), PyMem_RawRealloc() and PyMem_RawFree() functions are used by the raw memory allocator.

■ PyMem_GetAllocator(), PyMem_SetAllocator() and PyMem_SetupDebugHooks() functions are used by the object specific allocators.

**2.9 Comparison Between C and Python**

■ Developing the code in C is complex; whereas it is easy in Python.

■ In C, indentation is not mandatory; whereas in Python, indentation is mandatory.

■ In C, each variable requires declaration; whereas in Python, declaration is not required for a variable.

■ In-line assignments are supported in C; whereas in Python, they are not supported.

■ In C, pointers can be used; whereas in Python, pointers are not used.

■ In C, garbage collector is not used; whereas in Python, garbage collector is used.

■ In C, there are no automatic memory management tools; whereas in Python, there are automatic memory management tools.

■ In C, the semicolon is used for ending the statements; whereas in Python, the statement is ends with new line character.

**2.10 Comparison Between Java and Python**

■ Developing the code in Java is complex; whereas it is easy in Python.

■ In Java indentation is not mandatory; whereas in Python, indentation is mandatory.

■ In Java, each variable requires declaration; whereas in Python, declaration is not required for a variable.

■ In Java, a class can have multiple constructors; whereas in Python, only one constructor is allowed in a class.

■ Handling strings in Java is weak; whereas handling strings in Python is strong.

■ Java uses static typing; whereas Python uses dynamic typing.

■ The execution speed of Java is higher than that in Python.

■ MATLAB

SAS contains a wide variety of functions for performing statistical operations. But SAS is not open-source software. To overcome this, R-programming was introduced. It is open-source software that can be distributed to anyone. The Python provides various functions for performing statistical operations and other operations also. MATLAB software is also popular and widely used software but it is not open source software.

**2.13 Python Identifiers**

The identifier represents the names used in the Python program. The length of the identifier can be varied from one to another. The rules for validating the identifier are listed below.

■ The identifier can be a combination of various elements viz., uppercase letters, lowercase letters, digits and underscore.

■ Any one of the keyword is not permitted to act as identifier.

■ The first letter of the identifier should not be a digit.

■ Special alphabets viz., !,@,# etc... are also not permitted to be a part of identifier.

In Python, the comments can be specified in any one of the following two ways.

■ “#” is used to represent the single line comment.

■ Multiple line comment statements are started and ended with the “”” delimiter.

**2.17 Differences Between Python 2.x and 3.x**

■ In Python 2.x, the print statement is used; whereas in 3.x, the print() function is used.

■ In Python 2.x, integer division returns only integer result; whereas in 3.x, it can return floating value. For example, for 3/2 operation, the result is 1 in 2.x while the result is 1.5 in 3.x.

■ The input() function of 3.x is same as raw_input() function in 2.x.

■ In 2.x, “file” command should be used to open the file; whereas in 3.x., “open()” function should be used.

■ The “xrange()” function of 2.x is the same as “range()” function in 3.x.

■ In 2.x, the implicit type string code is ASCII; whereas in 3.x, the implicit string code is UNICODE.

■ In Python 3.x, additional “as” keyword should be used for handling exception; whereas in 2.x, it is not required.

■ _future_module is required to upgrade from Python 2.x to 3.x.

**2.18 Coding Styles in Python**

In Python, there are four different types of coding styles. They are

■ Functional

■ Imperative

■ Procedural

■ Object oriented

**Functional**

In this style of coding, the given problem is decomposed into various functions. It is widely used for lambda calculus.

![image](images/9789386235633_pt.png) **Programming Tip:**

In Python, print() is used for printing the input value on the monitor.

import functools

M = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

res = functools.reduce(lambda a, b: a + b, M)

print(“result=”,res)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

result=150

In the above sample code, “functools” module is used. This module is used for higher-order functions. The “lambda” is used to create the anonymous functions at runtime. The “reduce()” is used to reduce the iterable to a single value.

**Imperative**

In this style of coding, the order of the operations should be defined explicitly. It is widely used for handling various operations of data structures.

M = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

res = 0

for a in M:

res=res+a

print(“result=”,res)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

result= 150

**Procedural**

In this style of coding, focus is given on the code. The given problem is described in terms of list of instructions. The code is efficiently handled with multiple functions. It uses iteration, modularization etc. for avoiding code redundancy.

def su(N):

res = 0

for a in N:

res=res+a

return(res)

M = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

print(“result=”,su(M))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

result= 150

**Object oriented**

In this style of coding, focus is given on the data. The data is considered as a real-time object. The code that works with the object data is grouped into a single entity and is called as a “class”. The class contains members and methods. Some of the object oriented principles are

■ **Abstraction**

Abstraction allows the programmer to use the object without knowing the details of the object.

■ **Inheritance**

Inheritance allows one class to derive the properties of the other class. With this, the properties of the base class are derived into sub-class.

■ **Encapsulation**

Encapsulation binds all the properties, viz., members and methods into single entity called as “class.”

■ **Polymorphism**

Polymorphism allows the same entity to be used in different forms. There are two types of polymorphism i) Compile-time polymorphism and ii) Run-time polymorphism.

class X:

def su(self,x,y):

return(x+y)

y=X();

print(“result=”,y.su(10,20))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

result= 30

In the above sample code, the class “X” contains method “su()”. For the class “X”, an object “y” is created.

**Glossary**

■ The Python Virtual Machine (PVM) is used to provide platform-independent feature for Python scripts.

**Summary**

■ In the present chapter, the advantages of Python programming language over other programming languages, viz., C and Java are discussed.

■ The Python language is platform-independent because of the use of Python Virtual Machine (PVM).

■ Indentation should be followed in Python programming.

**4.** True

**5.** False

**6.** False

**9.** True

**10.** False

**11.** True

**15.** False

**20.** True

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Syntax and Styles** |
| **3** |   |

In this chapter, we discuss the syntax and styles used in Python programming language. Based on the initialized value, the data type will be automatically assigned to the variable. The values are represented by literals. Like other programming languages, Python also supports various operators and expressions.

**3.1 Data Types**

In Python programming language, the data can be maintained by variables. The type of variable represents the value stored within the variable. Type inferencing is allowed in Python. So, general keywords like int, float etc. are not required for the variable.

D:\Python>python

Python 3.6.0 (v3.6.0:41df79263a11, Dec 23 2016, 07:18:10) [MSC v.1900 32 bit (Intel)] on win32

Type “help”, “copyright”, “credits” or “license” for more information.

>>> 10

10

>>> 35.76

35.76

>>> ‘abcd’

‘abcd’

>>> 1,00,000

(1, 0, 0)

In Python, type keyword is used to get the data type assigned to the input data.

>>> type(100)

<class ‘int’>

>>> type(15.27)

<class ‘float’>

>>> type(‘abcd’)

<class ‘str’>

The bool data type contains two possible values viz., “True” and “False”.

>>> bool

<class ‘bool’>

The “False” value can be considered in any one of the following cases.

■ If the value is “None”

■ If the value is “0” or “0.0”

■ If the input value is an empty list, tuple or string.

>>> bool(‘a’)

True

>>> bool(‘’)

False

**3.2 Literal**

A literal is a value that can be parsed and turned into an object. In Python, literals can be any one of the following.

■ Int

■ Complex

■ Float

■ String

■ Unicode

■ List

■ Tuple

■ dict literal

**3.3 Numeric Literal**

■ The integer literal converts the input integer value to int object.

>>> type(12)

<class ‘int’>

■ The float literal converts the input float value to float object.

>>> type(12.5)

<class ‘float’>

■ The complex literal converts the input complex value to complex object.

>>> type(2+5j)

<class ‘complex’>

**3.4 Boolean Literal**

The Boolean literal will convert either True or False value to bool object.

>>> type(True)

<class ‘bool’>

>>> type(False)

<class ‘bool’>

**3.5 String Literal**

The string value defined in single quote or double quotes can be considered as string literal. The string literal can convert the input string value to a string object.

>>> type(‘hello’)

<class ‘str’>

>>> type(“hello”)

<class ‘str’>

**3.6 Variable**

In Python, variables can be initialized without declaration. Based on the value of the input data, the type of the variable will be automatically considered. A variable name should start with a letter followed by sequence of letters, numbers or underscore (_) special character.

Integer data, floating point data and string data will be considered as primitive data type. Character data will be considered as string data with length 1.

>>> a=65

>>> b=78.24

>>>c=’hai’

>>>a

65

>>>b

78.24

>>>c

‘hai’

In Python, the string data will be considered as an array. The array index will start from 0.

>>> a=’chapter-2’

>>> a

‘chapter-2’

>>> a[1]

‘h’

>>> a[0]

‘c’

>>> a[5]

‘e’

For string data, ‘+’ represents the concatenation operation and ‘*’ represents the repetition operation.

>>> a=(‘ha’+’i’)

>>> a

‘hai’

>>> a=(‘ha’*2)

>>> a

‘haha’

Other than the primitive variables, Python also supports the following variables.

**Tuples **

A tuple is a list with a fixed number of elements. It uses parentheses for specification of data.

>>> x=(4,5,6)

>>> x[0]

4

>>> x[1]

5

>>> x[-1]

6

>>> x[2]

6

The index ‘-1’ represents the last element in the tuple. In case of tuple variable, change of values is not permitted.

>>> x[2]=10

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object does not support item assignment

**Lists  **

Lists are different from tuples. A list does not have a fixed number of elements. It uses square brackets for specification of data. The change of values of a list variable is allowed.

>>> x=[4,5,6]

>>> x[0]

4

>>> x[1]

5

>>> x[-1]

6

>>> x[2]

6

>>> x[0]=10

>>> x

[10, 5, 6]

**Dictionaries **

It is a variable used to maintain multiple typed elements. It uses curly braces for specification of data. It maintains the data by using {key:value,...} format. Multiple entries are separated by the comma operator. The change of values is allowed.

>>> x={1:’a’,2:2,3:5.8}

>>> x

{1: ‘a’, 2: 2, 3: 5.8}

>>> x[1]

‘a’

>>> x[2]

2

>>> x[3]

5.8

>>> x[1]=’b’

>>> x

{1: ‘b’, 2: 2, 3: 5.8}

**3.7 Operators and Expressions**

The Python programming language supports the following operators.

■ Arithmetic operators

■ Comparison (Relational) operators

■ Assignment operators

■ Logical operators

■ Bitwise operators

■ Membership operators

■ Identity operators

**3.7.1 Arithmetic operators**

Arithmetic operators are used to perform arithmetic operations between any two operands. The various arithmetic operators supported by Python are given below:

■ The ‘+’ operator is used to perform the addition operation between the two input data values.

■ The ‘-’ operator is used to perform the subtraction operation between the two input data values.

■ The ‘*’ operator is used to perform the multiplication operation between the two input data values.

■ The ‘/’ operator is used to perform the division operation between the two input data values.

■ The ‘%’ operator is used to return the remainder of the division operation performed between the two input data values.

■ The ‘**’ operator is used for calculating the exponential.

■ The ‘//’ operator is used to perform the floor division operation between the two input data values. It returns the quotient of the division operation. The decimal points in the quotient result will be removed. If any one of the input is negative, then the quotient result is rounded towards negative infinity.

For example, if the two numbers considered are 20 and 10, then the result of

a + b = 30

a - b = 10

a * b = 200

a / b = 2

a % b = 0 (remainder is 0)

a ** 2 = 400 (202)

-a//b = -2 (a is negative, so the quotient will be rounded towards negative infinity)

**3.7.2 Comparison (relational) operators**

These operators are used to estimate the relation between two given input values. The various relational operators are given below:

■ The ‘==’ operator is used to compare whether the two given input values are equal or not. It returns True when both are equal; otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘!=’ operator is used to compare whether the two given input values are equal or not. It returns True when both are not equal; otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘>’ operator is used to compare the two given input values, whether the left side value is greater than right side value or not. It returns True if the left side value is greater than the other value otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘<’ operator is used to compare the two given input values, whether the left side value is less than right side value or not. It returns True if the left side value is less than the other value otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘>=’ operator is used to compare the two given input values, whether the left side value is greater than or equal to right side value or not. It returns True if the left side value is greater than or equal to the other value otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘<=’ operator is used to compare the two given input values, whether the left side value is less than or equal to right side value or not. It returns True if the left side value is less than or equal to the other value otherwise it returns False.

For example, if the two numbers considered are 20 and 10 then the result of

| (a == b) | → | False |
| (a != b) | → | True |
| (a > b) | → | True |
| (a < b) | → | False |
| (a >= b) | → | True |
| (a <= b) | → | False |

**3.7.3 Assignment operators**

The assignment operator is used to assign the input value to the variable. The assignment operator can be grouped with arithmetic operators and are called as compound assignment operators. The various assignment operators are as follows:

■ The ‘=’ operator is used to assign the input value (right-side value) to the variable (left-side variable).

■ The ‘+=’ is used to add the right-side value and the left-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

■ The ‘-=’ is used to subtract the right-side value from the left-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

■ The ‘*=’ is used to multiply the right-side value and the left-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

■ The ‘/=’ is used to divide the left-side value by the right-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

■ The ‘**=’ is used to compute the exponential of the left-side value raised to the right-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

■ The ‘//=’ is used to compute the result of floor division of the left-side value by the right-side value and assigns the result to the left-side variable.

The results of the following operators are

| (a = 20) | → | value of a is 20 |
| (a = 20), (a += 10) | → | value of a is 30 |
| (a = 20), (a -= 10) | → | value of a is 10 |
| (a = 20), (a *= 10) | → | value of a is 200 |
| (a = 20), (a /= 10) | → | value of a is 2 |
| (a = 20), (a **= 2) | → | value of a is 400 |
| (a = 20), (a //= 10) | → | value of a is -2 |

**3.7.4 Logical operators**

The logical relation between two input values is computed by logical operators. The various logical operators are

■ The ‘and’ is called as Logical AND operator. It returns True only when both the inputs are True.

■ The ‘or’ is called as Logical OR operator. It returns True when at least one of input is True.

■ The ‘not’ is called as Logical NOT operator. It returns the negativity of the input value.

For example, if two input numbers a and b are 1 and 0, then the results of the following operations are

| (a and b) | → | False |
| (a or b) | → | True |
| (not (a)) | → | False |

**3.7.5 Bitwise operators**

The bitwise operators work bit by bit, on the input data value. For each value, the 8-bit ASCII code will be considered and on these bits, the following operations are performed.

■ The ‘&’ is called as binary AND operation. It will select the resultant bit to True only when the corresponding two input bit values are True; otherwise it is False.

■ The ‘|’ is called as binary OR operation. It will select the resultant bit to True when at least one of the corresponding two input bit values are True; otherwise it is False.

■ The ‘^’ is called as binary XOR operation. It will select the resultant bit to True only when the corresponding two input bit values are different; otherwise it is False.

■ The ‘~’ is called as binary ones complement operation. It will set the resultant bit to the complemented input bit value.

■ The ‘<<’ is called as binary Left Shift Operation. It will left shift the left-side value. The right-side value will specify the required number of shift operations.

■ The ‘>>’ is called as binary Right Shift Operation. It will right shift the left-side value. The right-side value will specify the required number of shift operations.

For example, if the two numbers considered are a = 101 and b = 011. The results of the following operations are

| (a & b) | → | 001 |
| (a &#124; b) | → | 111 |
| (a ^ b) | → | 110 |
| (~ a) | → | 010 |

**3.7.6 Membership operators**

The membership operators in Python are used for strings, lists or tuples. They focus on testing the membership in the sequence. The various operators are

■ The ‘in’ operator is used to search the input element in the sequence. If the input element is found, then it returns True; otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘not in’ operator is used to search the input element in the sequence. If the input element is not found then returns it True; otherwise it returns False.

x=(1,2,3)

y=2

a=(y in x)

print(“in operator result=”,a)

b=(y not in x)

print(“not in operator result=”,b)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms\aa>python a2.py

in operator result= True

not in operator result= False

**3.7.7 Identity operators**

Identity operators are used to evaluate whether the given two objects point to the same memory location or not. The various operators are

■ The ‘is’ operator is used to return True if both objects points to the same memory location; otherwise it returns False.

■ The ‘is not’ operator is used to return True if both objects are not pointing to the same memory location; otherwise it returns False.

x=(1,2,3)

y=2

a=(y is x)

print(“is operator result=”,a)

b=(y is not x)

print(“not is operator result=”,b)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms\aa>python a2.py

is operator result= False

not is operator result= True

**3.8 Expressions and Order of Evaluations**

Atom is a basic element of Python expression. An atom can be an identifier or literal. An Atom also consists of various forms enclosed in brackets, parentheses or braces. Name is an atom representing an identifier. Both the Byte and String literals are allowed in Python. Multiple expressions are separated by comma operator in a parenthesized form. The order of precedence of operators is evaluated based on the following [Table 3.1](#ch03.xhtml_tab3.1).

**Table 3.1** Operator Precedence in Python.

| **S. No.** | **Operator and Description** |
| 1 | ****** |
| 2 | **~ + -** |
| 3 | *** / % //** |
| 4 | **+ -** |
| 5 | **>> <<** |
| 6 | **&** |
| 7 | **^ &#124;** |
| 8 | **<= < > >=** |
| 9 | **<> == !=** |
| 10 | **= %= /= //= -= += *= **=** |
| 11 | Identity operators |
| 12 | Membership operators |
| 13 | Logical operators |

**3.9 Numbers**

In Python programming, when numerical values are assigned to a variable, the numeric objects are automatically created. The ‘del’ keyword is used to delete reference to the numeric objects. The syntax of ‘del’ keyword is

del numericalobject-1[,......,numericalobject-n]

■ If the ‘del’ keyword is applied on the numeric object where it is not initialized, then a runtime error will be generated.

b=20

print(“Value of B=”,b)

del(a)

print(“Value of A=”,a)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python del.py

Value of B= 20

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “del.py”, line 3, in <module>

del(a)

NameError: name ‘a’ is not defined

In Python programming language, there are three types of numerical objects. They are

  i. integers

 ii. floating point numbers

iii. complex numbers

**3.9.1 Integers**

Integers represent fixed point numerical data. So, Python maintains integer numbers with no decimal point. In early versions, there were integers and long integers. However, in Version3, only int with unlimited size is allowed. With this, both the positive and negative numbers can be represented. A subset of integers is allowed to represent the Boolean values. An integer value can be represented in binary, decimal, hexa-decimal or octal form.

>>> a=10   #decimal integer value

>>> a

10

>>> a=0b100   #binary integer value

>>> a

4

>>> a=0x16   #hexa-decimal integer value

>>> a

22

>>> a=0o24   #octal integer value

>>> a

20

**3.9.2 Floating point numbers**

Floating point numbers are used to represent floating point numerical data. So, it maintains real numbers with decimal point separating the integer and fractional parts. Scientific notation may also be used to represent the data. The exponential power is represented with ‘E’ or ‘e’ in the decimal system.

**3.9.3 Complex numbers**

Complex numbers are used to represent the numerical data in terms of *x* + i*y*, where *x* represents the real part and *y* represents the imaginary part. Both *x* and *y* values are floating point numbers.

**3.10 Functions Applicable to All Types of Numbers**

In Python programming, one type of numerical data can be converted to another type of data by using number type conversion functions. Common number type conversion functions are listed below.

■ int() function will convert and return the input data to a integer number. It requires one argument.

■ long() function will convert and return the input data to a long integer number. It requires one argument.

■ float() function will convert and return the input data to a real number. It requires one argument.

■ complex() will convert and return the input data to a complex number. It can take one or two arguments. If one argument is provided, then it will be placed in real value with zero imaginary value. If two are provided, then they will be considered for both real and imaginary values.

Various mathematical functions are defined in math module and are listed below.

■ The ceil(arg1) function will return the ceiling value of the argument (arg1)

■ The fabs(arg1) function will return the absolute value of the input float arg1.

■ The abs(arg1) function will return the absolute value of the input integer arg1.

■ The floor(arg1) function will return the floor value of the arg1.

■ The copysign(arg1,arg2) function will copy the sign of *y* into *x*.

■ The isinf(arg1) function will return the TRUE if arg1 is infinity; otherwise it returns FLASE.

■ The isnan(arg1) function will return the TRUE if arg1 is a NaN; otherwise it returns FLASE.

■ The exp(arg1) function returns the exponential value of arg1.

■ The log2(arg1) function returns the log2 value of arg1.

■ The pow(arg1,arg2) function returns the arg1arg2 value.

■ The acos(arg1) function returns the arc cosine value of arg1.

■ The sin(arg1) function returns the sine value of arg1.

■ The atan(arg1) function returns the arc tangent value of arg1.

■ The radians(arg1) function returns the radian value of arg1(degrees)

■ pi is a mathematical constant used to represent the π value

■ e is a mathematical constant used to represent the constant value of ‘e’

**Glossary**

■ Literal is a value which can be parsed and further turned into an object.

■ A variable can be initialized with a value without declaration in Python

■ Operator indicates the operation to be performed on the operands.

**Summary**

■ In Python, int, string and float are considered as primitive data types. Other than these, tuples, lists and dictionaries are also considered as primitive data type.

■ Literals are converted to primitive data objects.

■ There are various operators to perform operations in the statement.

■ The Python programming supports integer, floating and complex numbers.

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Control Flow** |
| **4** |   |

This chapter discusses about the decision making statements and looping statements. Decision making statements evaluate the condition and execute the associated block of statements for a single time. Looping statements evaluate the condition and execute the associated block of statements repeatedly until the condition has failed.

**4.1 If Statement**

In Python programming, decision making statements are used to identify the set of codes to be executed according to the given conditions. The condition will be evaluated and the result can be either TRUE or FALSE. Any non-null or non-zero value will be considered as TRUE and any zero or null value will be considered as FALSE. There are three types of decision making statements. They are

  i. if statement

 ii. if..else statement

iii. nested if statement

![image](images/9789386235633_14.png)

**Figure 4.1** Flowchart for if statement.

The flowchart for if statement is illustrated in [Fig. 4.1](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.1). For the if statement, only one condition is allowed. When the flow of control reaches the if statement, the condition given in the if statement is evaluated. If the evaluated result of the condition is FALSE, then the flow of control will not enter into the if statement block but continue the execution of Set 2 of statements. If the evaluated result of the condition is TRUE, then the flow of control enters into the if statement block and executes the Set 1 of statements. After completing the execution of the if statement block, the flow of control will continue the execution of Set 2 of statements.

If statement is used to evaluate the given condition and the block of statements is executed only if the condition is TRUE. If the condition is FALSE, then the block of statements will not be executed. For if statements, only one block of statements are allowed. The syntax is

if(condition):

#set of statements to be executed when the condition is TRUE

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number:”))

if(a>0):

print(“The given number is positive”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number:4

The given number is positive

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number:-1

In the above example program, initially, the value of ‘a’ is read followed by print “The given number is positive” statement. The if statement is used to check the given condition. If the condition (a>0) is true, then the statement will be printed. If the condition is FALSE, then nothing will be printed.

**Example 1**: Write a Python program to check whether a given character is a vowel or not.

a=input(“Enter the character:”)

if((a==’a’) or (a==’e’) or (a==’i’) or (a==’o’) or (a==’u’)):

print(“The given character is vowel”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the character: e

The given character is vowel

a=int(input(“Enter the given number:”))

if(a<5):

print(“square of the number:”,a*a)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the given number:3

square of the number: 9

a=int(input(“Enter the given number:”))

if((a%10)==0):

print(“The given number is divisible by 10”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the given number:100

The given number is divisible by 10

**Example 4:** Write a Python program to count the number of 100-rupee notes required for a given amount and print whether or not more than one 100-rupee note is required.

a=int(input(“Enter the Amount:”))

x=a/100;

if(x>1):

print(“More than one 100-rupee note required”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the Amount:200

More than one 100-rupee note required

The problem of if statement is that no block of statements will be executed when the condition is FALSE. To overcome this problem, the if..else statement is used.

![image](images/9789386235633_15.png)

**Figure 4.2** Flowchart for if-else statement

The flowchart for if-else statement is illustrated in [Fig. 4.2](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.2). For the if-else statement, only one condition is allowed. When the flow of control reaches the if-else statement, then the condition given in the if-else statement is evaluated. If the evaluated result of the condition is FALSE, then the flow of control will enter into the FALSE block and execute the Set 1 of statements and then continue the execution of Set-3 of statements. If the evaluated result of the condition is TRUE, then the flow of control enters into the TRUE block and executes Set 2 of statements and then continues the execution of Set 3 of statements. So, irrespective of the condition for the if-else statement, only one block, i.e., either TRUE or FALSE block will be executed and then Set 3 of statements will be executed finally.

In if..else statement, two blocks of statements (TRUE block and FALSE block) can be used. The condition is specified within the if statement. Initially, the condition will be evaluated and if the condition is TRUE, then the TRUE block will be evaluated. If the condition is FALSE, then the FALSE block will be evaluated. The syntax is

if(condition):

# TRUE block of statements

else:

# FALSE block of statements

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number:”))

if(a>0):

print(“The given number is positive”)

else:

print(“The given number is negative”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number:6

The given number is positive

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number:-2

The given number is negative

In the above example, only the condition for if statement is given. So, the drawback of if..else statement is that it can only evaluate one condition.

**Example 5**: Write a Python program to check if the given character is a vowel or consonant.

a=input(“Enter the character:”)

if((a==’a’) or (a==’e’) or (a==’i’) or (a==’o’) or (a==’u’)):

print(“The given character is vowel”)

else:

print(“The given character is consonant”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the character: b

The given character is consonant

**Example 6**: Write a Python to check if the given number is greater than 5\. If the number is greater than 5, then print the cube of the number. If the number is less than or equal to 5, then print the square of the number.

a=int(input(“Enter the given number:”))

if(a>5):

print(“Cube of the number:”, a*a*a)

else:

print(“Square of the number:”,a*a)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the given number:6

Cube of the number: 216

**Example 7**: Write a Python program to check the profit or loss.

a=int(input(“Enter the cost price:”))

b=int(input(“Enter the selling price:”))

if(b>a):

print(“Profit”)

else:

print(“Loss”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the selling price: 75

Loss

**Example 8**: Write a Python program to check whether the given character is an uppercase letter or a lower case letter.

a=input(“Enter the character:”)

if(a.islower()):

print(“the given character is lower case letter”)

else:

print(“the given character is upper case letter”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

Enter the character: V

The given character is upper case letter

To overcome this problem if..elif..else statement is used. In if..elif..else statement, multiple conditions can be specified for if and multiple elif statements. The else will be executed only when all the conditions are evaluated to be FALSE. The syntax for if..elif..else statement is

if(condition1):

# TRUE block1 of statements

elif(condition2):

# TRUE block2 of statements

else:

# FALSE block of statements

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number:”))

if((a>90)and(a<=100)):

print(“The given number is between 90 and 100”)

elif((a>80)and(a<=90)):

print(“The given number is between 80 and 90”)

elif((a>70)and(a<=80)):

print(“The given number is between 70 and 80”)

elif((a>60)and(a<=70)):

print(“The given number is between 60 and 70”)

elif((a>50)and(a<=60)):

print(“The given number is between 50 and 60”)

else:

print(“The given number is below 50”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 69

The given number is between 60 and 70

In the above example, if any one of the condition is evaluated as TRUE, then the corresponding TRUE block of statements will be executed but no other conditions will be evaluated. The nested if statement overcomes this problem by allowing if, if..else and if..elif..else statements with if/elif/else statements. The syntax is

if(condition1):

# TRUE block1 of statements

if(condition2):

# TRUE block2 of statements

.....

else:

# FALSE block1 of statements

if(condition3):

# TRUE block3 of statements

.....

![image](images/9789386235633_16.png)

**Figure 4.3** Flowchart for nested if statement.

The flowchart of nested if statement is illustrated in [Fig. 4.3](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.3). For the nested if statement, multiple conditions are allowed. When the flow of control reaches the nested if statement, then the condition1 given in the if statement will be evaluated. If the evaluated result of the condition is FALSE, then the flow of control will enter the FALSE block and Condition 2 will be evaluated. If the Condition 2 is evaluated as FALSE then the Set 1 of statements will be executed. If the Condition 2 is evaluated as TRUE, then the Set 2 of statements will be executed. After executing either Set 1 or Set 2 of statements, the flow of control is continued to execute the Set 5 of statements.

If Condition 1 is evaluated as TRUE, then another corresponding Condition 3 will be evaluated. If the Condition 3 is evaluated as FALSE, then Set 3 of statements will be executed. If the Condition 3 is evaluated as TRUE, then the Set 4 of statements will be executed. After executing Set 3 or Set 4 of statements, the flow of control is continued to execute the Set 5 of statements.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

if(a>0):

print(“first true block”)

if(a>10):

print(“second true block”)

else:

print(“first false block”)

else:

print(“second false block”)

if(a>-10):

print(“third true block”)

else:

print(“third false block”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 15

first true block

second true block

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 4

first true block

first false block

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: -3

second false block

third true block

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: -34

second false block

third false block

**4.2 While Statement**

In addition to decision making statements, Python programming also supports looping statements. There are two loops in Python. They are i) while and ii) for.

The while loop checks the condition initially and repeats the execution of the block of statements until the evaluated condition becomes False. The syntax for the while loop is

while(condition):

#block of statements

The condition is evaluated initially and if the condition is TRUE, then it will execute the block of statements. After this, the condition is evaluated again and the process is repeated until the condition becomes False.

![image](images/9789386235633_17.png)

**Figure 4.4** Flowchart for while loop

The flowchart of while loop is illustrated in [Fig. 4.4](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.4). For the while loop, one condition will be evaluated multiple times. Each time, when the condition becomes TRUE, the flow of control enters into the while block and executes the Set 1 of statements whereas when the condition becomes FALSE, the repeated evaluation of the condition and execution of the while block is terminated. Thus, when the condition becomes FALSE, the flow of control executes the Set 2 of statements.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

i=0

print(“Numbers from 0 to “,a)

while(i<a):

print(i)

i=i+1

***Output***

Enter the number: 5

Numbers from 0 to 5

0

1

2

3

4

a=int(input(“Enter the value of n:”))

x=0

while(a>0):

a=int(a/10)

x=x+1

print(“No of Digits:”,x)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the value of n:87963

No of Digits: 5

a=int(input(“Enter the value of n:”))

x=0

s=0

pr=1

while(a>0):

t=a

a=int(a/10)

di=t%10

s=s+di

pr=pr*di

print(“Sum of Digits:”,s)

print(“Product of Digits:”,pr)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the value of n: 54

Sum of Digits: 9

Product of Digits: 20

s=0

pr=1

t=a

a=int(a/10)

di=t%10

s=(s*10)+di

print(“Reverse of the number:”,s)
