**4.3 For Statement**

The for loop is used to repeat the execution of a block of code for a fixed number of times. In Python, the for loop is repeated for all the members of a sequence in order, executing the block each time, whereas in while loop the condition is evaluated for each iteration. The syntax of for loop is

for variable in range(val1,val2,inr):

#block of statements

In the above syntax val1 and val2 represents minimum and maximum number of the specified range. The ‘inr’ specifies the increment value.

![image](images/9789386235633_18.png)

**Figure 4.5** Flowchart for for loop

The flowchart of for loop is illustrated in [Fig. 4.5](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.5). In the for loop, one condition will be evaluated multiple times. Each time, when the condition becomes TRUE, then flow of control enters into the for loop block and executes the Set 1 of statements, whereas when the condition becomes FALSE, the repeated evaluation of condition and execution of for loop block is terminated. Thus, when the condition becomes FALSE, the flow of control executes the Set 2 of statements.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

for i in range(a):

print(i)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 5

0

1

2

3

4

In the above example, only one value is given in the range. This will be considered as maximum value of the range.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

for i in range(1,a):

print(i)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 4

1

2

3

In the above example, the minimum and maximum value of the range is specified. The ‘inr’ value is not specified; it is automatically assigned as 1.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

for i in range(1,a,2):

print(i)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 10

1

3

5

7

9

**4.4 Break Statement**

In Python programming, loops are used to execute a block of statements repeatedly. The body of the loop will be executed when the condition of the loop is evaluated as TRUE. During the execution of the loop, if an emergency exit from the loop is required then break statement is used. The break statement is used to quit the running loop statements. The syntax of the break statement is

loop (condition):

#block of statements

break

else:

#block of statements

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

i=0

while(i<a):

print(“Entered into while loop with value of i=”,i)

if(i==2):

break

i=i+1

else:

print(“break is not executed”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 5

Entered into while loop with value of i= 0

Entered into while loop with value of i= 1

Entered into while loop with value of i= 2

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 2

Entered into while loop with value of i= 0

Entered into while loop with value of i= 1

break is not executed

In case no break statement of the running loop is executed, then the else part of the loop will be executed.

**4.5 Continue Statement**

The continue statement is used to skip the current iteration of the loop. If the condition of the loop is evaluated as TRUE, then the body of the loop is executed. During the execution, the continue statement stops the current execution of the body and continues the next iteration. The syntax is

loop(condition):

#block of statements

continue

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

i=0

while(i<a):

if(i==2):

i=i+1

continue

else:

print(“Entered into while loop with value of i=”,i)

i=i+1

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 5

Entered into while loop with value of i= 0

Entered into while loop with value of i= 1

Entered into while loop with value of i= 3

Entered into while loop with value of i= 4

**4.6 Pass Statement**

The pass statement can be called as a do-nothing statement. It does not perform any action. This statement is mainly used to keep the CPU busy for some time. The ‘pass’ keyword is used for specifying the pass statement.

An example is

a=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

i=0

while(i<a):

if(i<=2):

i=i+1

pass

else:

print(“Entered into while loop with value of i=”,i)

i=i+1

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 5

Entered into while loop with value of i= 3

Entered into while loop with value of i= 4

In this program, the pass statement is used in while loop. During the iteration, the pass statement will not execute anything and looks similar to the continue statement. However, there is a difference between continue and pass statements. The continue statement will skip the execution of current iteration and save the execution time, whereas the pass statement will waste the execution time.

**4.7 Entry Controlled Loop**

Entry controlled loops evaluate the condition at the time of entry into the loop. The flowchart for entry controlled loop is shown in [Fig. 4.6](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.6). While loop and for loop are examples for entry controlled loop.

![image](images/9789386235633_19.png)

**Figure 4.6** Flowchart for entry controlled loop

**4.8 Exit Controlled Loop**

Exit controlled loops evaluate the condition at the time of exiting from the loop. The flow chart for exit controlled loop is shown in [Fig. 4.7](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.7). Do-while loop is considered as an exit controlled loop.

![image](images/9789386235633_20.png)

**Figure 4.7** Flowchart for exit controlled loop

In Python, do-while loop should be simulated with an entry controlled loop as shown in the example below.

a=0

while(1):

print(“hai”)

a=a+1

if(a>3):

break

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

hai

hai

hai

hai

**4.9 Counter Controlled Loop**

Counter controlled loops are loops which can iterate based on the counter value. If the counter value is reached, then the loop will be terminated; otherwise, the loop will be repeated. The flowchart is shown in [Fig. 4.8](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.8).

![image](images/9789386235633_21.png)

**Figure 4.8** Flowchart for counter controlled loop

For the counter controlled loop, the condition indicates whether the maximum value of the counter is reached or not. If the maximum value of the counter is reached, then the loop is terminated; otherwise, the flow of control enters into the loop and executes the block of statements within the loop.

for a in [‘a’,’e’,’i’,’o’,’u’]:

print(a,” is a vowel”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

a is a vowel

e is a vowel

i is a vowel

o is a vowel

u is a vowel

**4.10 Condition Controlled Loop**

Condition controlled loops are loops which can iterate based on the specified condition. If the condition is evaluated as FALSE, then the loop is terminated; otherwise, the loop is repeated. The flowchart is shown in [Fig. 4.9](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.9). For the condition controlled loop, the condition is evaluated as TRUE or FALSE.

![image](images/9789386235633_22.png)

**Figure 4.9** Flowchart for counter controlled loop

**4.11 Nested Loops**

Python allows the usage of nested loops. A nested loop allows the usage of one loop inside another loop. Thus, in a for loop, another for or while loop is allowed. Similarly, within a while loop, another for or while loop is allowed. The flowchart for nested loops is shown in [Fig. 4.10](#ch04.xhtml_fig4.10).

![image](images/9789386235633_23.png)

**Figure 4.10** Flowchart for nested loops

Various examples are given below.

1.

for a in range(3):

for b in range(3):

print(a,”,”,b)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

0 , 0

0 , 1

0 , 2

1 , 0

1 , 1

1 , 2

2 , 0

2 , 1

2 , 2

**2**.

i=0

while(i < 3):

j = 0

while(j <3):

print(i,”,”,j)

j=j+1

i = i + 1

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

0 , 0

0 , 1

0 , 2

1 , 0

1 , 1

1 , 2

2 , 0

2 , 1

2 , 2

**3**.

i=1

while(i < 5):

j=1

while(j<=i):

print(“* “,end=””)

j=j+1

print(“\r”)

i = i + 1

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

*

* *

* * *

* * * *

**4**.

x=5

m = 2*x - 2

for a in range(0, x):

for b in range(0, m):

print(end=” “)

for b in range(0, a+1):

print(“* “, end=””)

m = m - 2

print(“\r”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python b1.py

            *

         * *

      * * *

   * * * *

* * * * *

4.13.1 Simple temperature converter

print(“The Equivalent temperature in Celsius=”,res)

elif(a==2):

b=float(input(“Enter temperature in Celsius:”))

res=(b*1.8)+32

print(“The Equivalent temperature in Fahrenheit=”,res)

else:

break

4.13.3 Generation of vowel alphabet pattern

a=input(“Enter the vowel character:”)

if(a[0]==”a”):

m=””

for i in range(0,7):

for j in range(0,7):

if (((j == 0 or j == 6) and i != 0 and i != 1) or

((i == 1) and (j == 1 or j == 5)) or

((i==0) and j > 1 and j < 5) or ((i==3) and j >0 and j < 7)):

m=m+”#”

else:

m=m+” “

m=m+”\n”

print(m)

elif(a[0]==”e”):

m=””

for i in range(0,7):

for j in range(0,7):

if ( ((i==0 or i==3 or i==6) and j >0 and j <7) or

((j==0) and i>=0 and i<=7)

):

m=m+”#”

else:

m=m+” “

m=m+”\n”

print(m)

elif(a[0]==”i”):

m=””

for i in range(0,7):

for j in range(0,7):

if ( ((i==0 or i==6) and j >=0 and j <=7) or

((j==3) and i>=0 and i<=7)

):

m=m+”#”

else:

m=m+” “

m=m+”\n”

print(m)

elif(a[0]==”o”):

m=””

for i in range(0,7):

for j in range(0,7):

if (((j == 1 or j == 5) and i != 0 and i != 6) or ((i == 0 or i == 6) and j > 1 and j < 5)):

m=m+”#”

else:

m=m+” “

m=m+”\n”

print(m)

elif(a[0]==”u”):

m=””

for i in range(0,7):

for j in range(0,7):

if ( ((i==6) and j >0 and j <7) or

((j==0 or j==6) and i>=0 and i<=7)

):

m=m+”#”

else:

m=m+” “

m=m+”\n”

print(m)

else:

print(“The given character is not a vowel alphabet”)

**Glossary**

■ Decision making statements are used to perform an operation based on evaluation of a given condition.

■ Loop statements are used for repeated execution of a code based on evaluation of the condition.

■ Break statement is used to terminate the execution of a loop.

**Summary**

■ A single-block decision can be made with if statement.

■ The if..else statement is used to handle two blocks with single condition.

■ Multiple conditions can be handled with the nested if statement.

■ Python allows two looping statements. The for and while loops are used in Python.

■ The break statement will stop the loop and the continue statement will skip the current iteration of the loop.

■ The pass statement is a do-nothing statement and is used to keep the CPU busy for some time.

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Sequences – Lists** |
| **5** |   |

In this chapter, we shall discuss about sequences in the Python programming language. The array is not a primitive data type in Python. There are six sequences in Python. The list is an ordered set of data elements. It is a mutable, i.e., changes are allowed on list elements.

**5.1 Arrays**

In Python, an array is used to represent a group of similar data values. It is similar to a list except that the list contains multiple dissimilar data values. In Python, array is not a built-in data type. So, an array module should be imported to use the array in the program. To declare an array use the array( ) function. This function takes two arguments. The first argument specifies the type code and the second argument specifies the elements of the array. Individual elements can be accessed with [ ] operator.

from array import *

a1 = array(‘i’,[1,2,3])

print(“Array: “,a1)

print(“First element of array: “,a1[0])

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python bb.py

Array: array(‘i’, [1, 2, 3])

First element of array: 1

**5.2 Sequences**

Python programming supports sequences to store multiple values to improve the efficiency of organization. There are six sequence types. They are

■ Strings

■ Lists

■ Tuples

■ Byte arrays

■ Buffers

■ Range objects

**String**

String is a collection of character data. It is also called as character array.

s=input(“Enter the String:”)

print(“The entered string is:”,s)

print(type(s))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the String:hello

The entered string is: hello

<class ‘str’>

**List**

List is similar to string. It is used to represent the ordered list of values.

x=[10,20,30]

print(“The numbers in the list:”)

for i in range(0,3):

print(x[i])

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The numbers in the list:

10

20

30

**Tuple**

Tuple is similar to list except that it is immutable. No changes are allowed on Tuple.

x=(10,20,30)

print(“The numbers in the Tuple:”)

for i in range(0,3):

print(x[i])

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The numbers in the Tuple:

10

20

30

**Byte**

Byte is a small range of integers between 0 and 256\. It is an immutable sequence; thus, no changes are allowed. Bytearray is a mutable sequence to represent byte.

>>> a=b”100”

>>> print(a)

b’100’

In the above example code, a byte object ‘a’ is initialized with value 100 and is displayed. In the example code given next, a byte array object is initialized with the byte object.

>>> a=bytes([10, 20, 30])

>>> a

b’\n\x14\x1e’

>>> b=bytearray(a)

>>> b

bytearray(b’\n\x14\x1e’)

**Buffer**

Buffer is a sequence used to maintain the object of the argument value. In Python 3, it is renamed as ‘memory view’. It mainly used to provide a buffer for maintaining the input value even by applying the slice operation. The slice operation is used to extract elements from a given input object.

import numpy

a = numpy.ndarray(1000000)

print(a.data)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

<memory at 0x058FFC60>

**Range object**

range( ) function is used to create a Range object. Generally, it is used in loops to maintain the range value. It is an immutable sequence.

print(“The numbers from 0 to 10 with increment 3:”)

for i in range(0,10,3):

print(i)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The numbers from 0 to 10 with increment 3:

0

3

6

9

**Dictionaries and sets**

Dictionaries and sets are not sequences. They are containers to maintain the sequential data. Dictionaries are used to maintain the values by using keys.

d={1:’ab’,2:’cd’,3:’ef’}

print(“The elements in the dictionary:”)

for i in range(1,4):

print(d[i])

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The elements in the dictionary:

ab

cd

ef

A set is an unordered set of list. It does not allow duplicated values. The set is a mutable sequence, while frozen set is an immutable set. Set is implemented by using hash table.

s={“ghi”,”abc”,”def”}

print(“The elements in the set:”)

print(s)

a=”ghi” in s

if(a==True):

print(“ghi is available in the set”)

else:

print(“ghi is not available in the set”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The elements in the set:

{‘ghi’, ‘abc’, ‘def’}

ghi is avaiable in the set

**5.3 Using Lists**

Python supports data structures with the help of sequences. Sequences mainly focus on efficient representation and maintenance of large data. Large data can be maintained in ordered and unordered form. If the data is maintained, say, according to serial number, then it is maintained in an ordered way. The list is similar to array in C, C++ or Java.

In Python, the list is represented with [] (square brackets). The list is created by providing values separated by “,”. A list can maintain homogeneous type of elements.

>>> x=[1,2,3]

>>> x

[1, 2, 3]

>>> x=[10.5,23.67,34.87,45.95]

>>> x

[10.5, 23.67, 34.87, 45.95]

>>> x=[“q”,”s”,”e”,”d”]

>>> x

[‘q’, ‘s’, ‘e’, ‘d’]

A list allows duplicated ordered set of values.

>>> li=[20,30,20,30]

>>> li

[20, 30, 20, 30]

A list contains an ordered set of elements. So, the elements of a list can be accessed by using the index. As with array, the index for list also starts with 0.

>>> l=[54,47,35,99]

>>> l[0]

54

>>> l[1]

47

>>> l[2]

35

>>> l[3]

99

>>> l[4]

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

IndexError: list index out of range

In a list, if the index value is greater than range of the list then an error will be generated. The index value can also be negative value. If the index value is negative, then it will be subtracted from the maximum index value of the list.

>>> l=[54,47,35,99]

>>> l[-1]

99

An empty list can be created in two different ways. An empty list is represented with [].

>>> a=list()

>>> a

[]

>>> b=[]

>>> b

[]

In list, the elements can be updated.

>>> l=[87,65,45,34]

>>> l[0]

87

>>> l[0]=10

>>> l

[10, 65, 45, 34]

**5.4 List Assignment and Equivalence**

The list assignment process is used to assign all elements of one list to another list. In this, memory will be allocated for the two list objects. So, if one list object element is updated then it will not reflect on another list.

i. By using list( ) function, one list can be copied into another list.

>>> a=[5,2,3]

>>> c=list(a)

>>> c

[5, 2, 3]

>>> c[0]=10

>>> c

[10, 2, 3]

>>> a

[5, 2, 3]

In the above example, memory is allocated for both a and c list objects.

To assign individual elements from one list to another, we can use “=” operator.

>>> l1=[“a”,”b”,”c”]

>>> l2=[“b”,”d”,”e”]

>>> l1

[‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’]

>>> l2

[‘b’, ‘d’, ‘e’]

>>> l2[1]=”x”

>>> l1

[‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’]

>>> l2

[‘b’, ‘x’, ‘e’]

>>> l1[0]=l2[0]

>>> l1

[‘b’, ‘b’, ‘c’]

>>> l2

[‘b’, ‘x’, ‘e’]

In the above example, two lists l1 and l2 are created with different elements. We updated a few elements of the lists. Then, element from l2 list was assigned to l1 list element.

Within a single statement, multiple variables can also be assigned with the list.

>>> l1,l3=[“ab”,”cd”]

>>> l1

‘ab’

>>> l3

‘cd’

The elements from list1 and list2 can also be compared to check whether they are equal. For this, Equivalent “==” operator is used. If both the lists are same, then it will return TRUE; otherwise it will return FALSE.

>>> a=[1,2,3]

>>> b=list(a)

>>> a

[1, 2, 3]

>>> b

[1, 2, 3]

>>> a==b

True

>>> a[0]=10

>>> a==b

False

An individual element of the list can also be compared with the another element by using the same operator.

>>> l1=[“a”,”b”,”a”,”c”]

>>> l1[0]==l1[1]

False

>>> l1[0]==l1[2]

True

Other than this operator, all the operators available in Python can be applied on elements of the list.

**5.5 List Bounds**

In list assignment, memory is allocated to both the list objects. So, updates made on one list object will not be reflected in another list. To overcome this problem, the list bound is used. In this process, only one copy of the list object is maintained in memory and is shared by all other multiple list objects. Thus, if one list object updates the content of the list, it will reflect in all other list objects.

>>> lis1=[10,20,30]

>>> lis2=lis1

>>> lis1

[10, 20, 30]

>>> lis2

[10, 20, 30]

>>> lis1[0]=100

>>> lis1

[100, 20, 30]

>>> lis2

[100, 20, 30]

**5.6 List Slicing**

List slicing is used to copy selected elements from one list to another list. By using [:], all elements of one list can be assigned to another list.

>>> c=[10,2,3]

>>> d=c[:]

>>> d

[10, 2, 3]

>>> d[1]=20

>>> d

[10, 20, 3]

>>> c

[10, 2, 3]

We can specify the start position and end position for slicing operator.

>>> lis1=[100,200,300,400,500,600,700,800,900]

>>> lis2=lis1

>>> lis2

[100, 200, 300, 400, 500, 600, 700, 800, 900]

>>> lis1

[100, 200, 300, 400, 500, 600, 700, 800, 900]

>>> lis3=lis1[1:3]

>>> lis3

[200, 300]

We can also specify an increment value for the slice operator. This is called as extending slices. In the below example code, the range is started from index 3 and the index is incremented by 2 each time until it reaches index 8.

>>> lis1=[100,200,300,400,500,600,700,800,900]

>>> lis1

[100, 200, 300, 400, 500, 600, 700, 800, 900]

>>> lis4=lis1[3:8:2]

>>> lis4

[400, 600, 800]

**5.7 List Cloning**

In Python, the cloning operation will copy the list from one object to another and the changes made to one list cannot be reflected to another list.

x=[1,2,3]

y=x

k=x[:]

z=x.copy()

print(«list x:»,x)

print(«list y:»,y)

print(“list k:”,k)

print(“list z:”,z)

print(“Results with assignment copy:”)

y[0]=10

print(«list x:»,x)

print(“list y:”,y)

print(“Results with clone:”)

k[0]=1000

print(“list x:”,x)

print(“list k:”,k)

print(“Results with copy():”)

z[0]=100

print(«list x:»,x)

print(«list z:»,z)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

list x: [1, 2, 3]

list y: [1, 2, 3]

list k: [1, 2, 3]

list z: [1, 2, 3]

Results with assignment copy:

list x: [10, 2, 3]

list y: [10, 2, 3]

Results with clone:

list x: [10, 2, 3]

list k: [1000, 2, 3]

Results with copy():

list x: [10, 2, 3]

list z: [100, 2, 3]

**5.8 Nested Lists**

In Python, a list can consist of another list(s) as elements. In this, the item can be accessed with the index of the sub-list and the index of the item in the sub-list.

x=[[1,2,3],[‘a’,’b’,’c’]]

for a in x:

for b in a:

print(b)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

1

2

3

a

b

c

**5.9 List Comprehensions**

In Python, list comprehension allows the programmer to define the list elements according to mathematical equations. For example, list comprehension for the equation ![image](images/9789386235633_102.png) Ais

>>> A = [y**2 for y in range(5)]

>>> print(A)

[0, 1, 4, 9, 16]

**5.10 Lists and Functions**

We can perform various operations on a list, as given below.

■ The del( ) function is used to delete the list element. The del( ) is also used to delete a list object. Once the list object is deleted, the memory allocated for the list is deallocated.

>>> lis4=[400,600,800]

>>> lis4

[400, 600, 800]

>>> del lis4[0]

>>> lis4

[600, 800]

>>> del lis4

>>> lis4

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

NameError: name ‘lis4’ is not defined

■ The len( ) function is used to return the length of the list object. It specifies the number of elements in the list.

>>> g=[9.56,6.45,8.94,2.78]

>>> len(g)

4

■ Two list objects can be concatenated or merged with the help of ‘+’ operator.

>>> g=[4.8,7.2,6.5,9.2]

>>> f=[1,2,3]

>>> e=f+g

>>> e

[1, 2, 3, 4.8, 7.2, 6.5, 9.2]

>>> g=[4.8,7.2,6.5,9.2]

>>> f=[1,2,3]

>>> h=g[2:5]+f[1:2]

>>> h

[6.5, 9.2, 2]

■ The list elements can be repeated by using the ‘*’ operator. For this, the factor of repetition should be provided.

>>> a=[444,77,22]

>>> b=a*3

>>> a

[444, 77, 22]

>>> b

[444, 77, 22, 444, 77, 22, 444, 77, 22]

■ The max( ) function will return the maximum element within the given list.

>>> k1=[6,3,9,23,1,4]

>>> max(k1)

23

>>> k2=[2.6, 30]

>>> max(k2)

30

>>> k3=[“a”,”b”,”c”]

>>> max(k3)

‘c’

■ The min( ) function will return the minimum element within the given list.

>>> k1=[6,3,9,23,1,4]

>>> k2=[2.6, 30]

>>> k3=[“a”,”b”,”c”]

>>> max(k3)

‘c’

>>> min(k1)

1

>>> min(k2)

2.6

>>> min(k3)

‘a’

■ The in keyword is used to test whether an item forms part of the list. It returns True when the item is available in the input list.

■ List Membership is also tested with the not in keyword. It returns True when the item is not available within the input list.

■ The sum( ) function returns the sum of all items in the input list.

■ The all( ) function checks whether the list contain all True values or not.

■ The any( ) function checks whether the list contains at least one True element.

■ The list( ) function will convert any iterable element to a list.

■ The sorted( ) function returns the sorted list.

**5.11 Prime Generation with a List**

A prime number is a number which can be divided by only by itself and 1\. To develop a program for finding the prime numbers within a series, list can be used.

a=int(input(“Enter the starting number of the series:”))

b=int(input(“Enter the ending number of the series:”))

l=list(range(a,b))

m=len(l)

f=[]

for j in range(m):

x=l[j]

flag=0;

for i in range(2,x):

if((x%i)==0):

flag=1

break

if(flag==0):

v=[x]

f=f+v

print(“The prime numbers:”,f)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the starting number of the series: 2

Enter the ending number of the series: 20

The prime numbers: [2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19]

In the code given above, initially the starting and ending values of the series are stored in two variables, a and b, respectively. Then list ‘l’ is used to maintain all the consecutive numbers between a and b. For this, range( ) is used. Then every number of the list ‘l’ is verified to check if it is a prime number. If the number is a prime number, then it is stored in a new list ‘f’. Whenever a new prime number is identified then it is concatenated to the previous ‘f’ list. Finally, the list ‘f’ is displayed.

To add the current number to the list, the following code is used.

a=[]

b=10

c=[b]

d=a+c

print(a)

print(b)

print(c)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

[]

10

[10]</O1>

**5.12 Adding List Elements**

In a list, new elements can be added by using built-in functions. These built-in functions are discussed below.

■ The append( ) method is used to add a new list as a sub-list of the current list object.

>>> l1=[“a”,”b”,”c”]

>>> l2=[“d”,”e”]

>>> l1.append(l2)

>>> l1

[‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’, [‘d’, ‘e’]]

>>> l1[0]

‘a’

>>> l1[1]

‘b’

>>> l1[2]

‘c’

>>> l1[3]

[‘d’, ‘e’]

>>> l1[3][0]

‘d’

>>> l1[3][1]

‘e’

■ The extend( ) method is similar to append( ) except that the new list elements are not appended as a sub-list.

>>> l1=[“a”,”b”,”c”]

>>> l2=[“d”,”e”]

>>> l1.extend(l2)

>>> l1

[‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’, ‘d’, ‘e’]

>>> l1[0]

‘a’

>>> l1[1]

‘b’

>>> l1[4]

‘e’

■ The count( ) method is used to count the frequency of occurrence of an element in the current list object.

>>> s=[4,8,1,9,2,4,1,7,1]

>>> s

[4, 8, 1, 9, 2, 4, 1, 7, 1]

>>> s.count(1)

3

>>> s.count(7)

1

■ The index( ) method will return the index of the input element within a list. If there are duplicated elements in a list, then the least index value will be returned.

>>> x=[“abc”,”def”,”abc”,”ghi”,”abc”]

>>> x.index(“abc”)

0

>>> x.index(“ghi”)

3

■ The inset( ) method is used to add the new list object to the current list object at the specified index position.

>>> x=[“abc”,”def”,”abc”,”ghi”,”abc”]

>>> x

[‘abc’, ‘def’, ‘abc’, ‘ghi’, ‘abc’]

>>> y=[«hello»]

>>> y

[‘hello’]

>>> x.insert(3,y)

>>> x

[‘abc’, ‘def’, ‘abc’, [‘hello’], ‘ghi’, ‘abc’]

■ The pop( ) method deletes the element at the given index in the list

■ The remove( ) method deletes the given element in the list.

■ The reverse( ) method is used to reverse the order of elements in the list.

■ The sort( ) method returns the sorted list in ascending order.

**5.13 Mutability**

In Python, mutability feature allows the data objects to be updated. Mutable objects can be modified with new elements whereas immutable elements do not accept any modifications of their data. For example, string objects are immutable objects and string modifications lead to error.

>>> s1=”def”

>>> s1

‘def’

>>> s1[0]

‘d’

>>> s1[0]=”a”

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘str’ object does not support item assignment

Various immutable data types are int, float, complex, string, tuple, frozenset and bytes. Various mutable data types are list, bytearray, set, dictionary, class and class instances.

List items are mutable objects. Hence, they can be modified.

>>> x=[5,1,9,2]

>>> x.sort()

>>> x

[1, 2, 5, 9]

>>> x.reverse()

>>> x

[9, 5, 2, 1]

>>> x.remove(9)

>>> x

[5, 2, 1]

>>> x.pop()

1

■ The sort( ) method is used to sort the elements of a current list object

■ The reverse( ) method is used to return the reverse list of a current list object.

■ The remove( ) method will remove the specified element in a current list object.

■ The pop( ) method will pop the top of the element in a stack

**5.14 List Unpacking**

List elements can be unpacked and stored in corresponding variables. To avoid assigning values in the input list, the *_ can be used.

(1, 2, 3)

2

y,z,*_=x

print(x)

print(y)

print(z)

**5.15 Stack**

A stack is a data structure used to maintain the data in Last-In–First-Out Order (LIFO). In the stack, two operations can be performed. They are push( ) and pop( ). The push( ) operation is used to insert a data element into the stack. The pop( ) operation is used to remove a data element from the stack. To perform the push( ) and pop( ) operations, the position is represented as TOP (Top of the Stack). Various operations of a stack are shown in [Fig. 5.1](#ch05.xhtml_fig5.1).

![image](images/9789386235633_24.png)

**Figure. 5.1** (a) Initial stack       (b) push(10,20,30)       (c) pop( )

In [Fig. 5.1 (a)](#ch05.xhtml_fig5.1), the original stack with no values is shown. When nothing is available inside the stack, then the value of TOP is -1\. If the element is pushed into the stack, then the TOP will be incremented and in the corresponding location, the value will be pushed as shown in [Fig. 5.1(b)](#ch05.xhtml_fig5.1). When the element is popped from the stack, then automatically the TOP will be decremented as shown in [Fig. 5.1(c)](#ch05.xhtml_fig5.1).

s=[]

top=-1

while(1):

print(“Menu for Stack operations:”)

print(“1\. Push”)

print(“2\. Pop”)

print(“3\. Display”)

print(“4\. Exit:”)

ch=int(input(“Enter the choice:”))

if(ch==1):

top=top+1

x=int(input(“Enter the number:”))

s.insert(top,x)

elif(ch==2):

y=s.pop()

print(“The popped element: “,y)

top=top-1

elif(ch==3):

print(“Stack:”,s)

elif(ch==4):

exit()

else:

print(“Invalid Choice:”)

**5.16 Queue**

A Queue is a data structure used to handle the data in the First-In–First-Out (FIFO) order. The data element can be inserted at one end of the queue and deleted from another end. The insertion position is represented with rear pointer and deletion is represented with front pointer.

q=[]

a=1

while a==1:

if(ch==1):

x=int(input(“Enter the number:”))

q.insert(0,x)

elif(ch==2):

y=q.pop()

elif(ch==3):

print(«Queue:»,q)

elif(ch==4):

exit()

else:

print(“Invalid Choice:”)

**5.17 Functional Programming**

In Python, functional programming is supported with three functions as listed below.

■ map( )

■ filter( )

■ reduce( )

map( ) **function**

The map( ) function will take the input list and applies the specified function on the list elements. It returns a list of elements.

a = [1,2,3,4]

b = [10,20,30,40]

c=list(map(lambda x,y:x+y, a,b))

print(“a= “,a)

print(“b= “,b)

print(“map() result= “,c)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a.py

a= [1, 2, 3, 4]

b= [10, 20, 30, 40]

map() result= [11, 22, 33, 44]

filter( ) **function**

The filter( ) function takes two arguments. The first argument is the function to be applied on the input list. The second argument is the list of elements on which the function should be performed. After generating the result, the filter( ) will filter only the elements which are True values.

a = [15,20,24,35]

c=list(filter(lambda x:x%2==0, a))

print(“a= “,a)

print(“filter() result= “,c)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a.py

a= [15, 20, 24, 35]

filter() result= [20, 24]

reduce( ) **function**

The reduce( ) function repeatedly applies itself on the input list of elements and finally generates a single value as the final result.

from functools import reduce

a = [15,20,24,35]

c=reduce(lambda x,y:x+y, a)

print(“a= “,a)

print(“filter() result= “,c)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a.py

a= [15, 20, 24, 35]

filter() result= 94

**Glossary**

■ An array represents a group of similar data values.

■ Sequences are used to store multiple dissimilar values.

■ Buffer sequence is used to maintain the object of an argument value.

■ List contains an ordered set of elements. List object is a mutable object.

**Summary**

■ There are six sequences in Python programming language, viz., string, lists, tuples, bytearray, buffer and range.

■ The present chapter discussed in detail about the properties of list.

■ Empty lists can be created in two different ways.

■ Various operations can be performed on the list object.

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Tuples** |
| **6** |   |

In addition to lists, Tuples are also efficient sequences in Python. While lists are mutable objects and tuples are immutable objects. So, updating and deleting operations are not allowed in tuples. The present chapter discusses in detail about tuples.

**6.1 Need of Tuple**

Tuple is similar to list except that it is immutable. That is, once the data is defined in tuple, then changes are not permitted on the data. The advantages of immutability are listed below:

■ During the copy operation of immutable objects, the efficiency is increases when compared with the mutable object.

■ During comparison operation of immutable objects, the efficiency is increases when compared with the mutable object.

■ For any immutable object value, only one copy can be stored.

■ During concurrent code, development of synchronized access to immutable objects is not required.

■ Supports const correctness feature.

■ Lists are used to maintain data that is dynamic whereas tuples are used to maintain data of fixed size.

■ For tuples, new elements cannot be added.

■ For tuples, the existing elements cannot be removed.

■ Tuples are used for maintaining the data by restricting add and remove features. Tuples allow only accessing of elements.

■ In general, constant values can be maintained with tuple.

■ Tuples can be executed faster than lists.

■ Tuples can also be allowed to act as keys of dictionary, whereas this is not allowed for lists.

■ The portability of lists is more than that of tuples.

Tuples is declared similar to the list except that it uses ().

>>> a=(4,”t”,6.5)

>>> a

(4, ‘t’, 6.5)

>>> type(a)

<class ‘tuple’>

Any object can be converted to tuple by using tuple( ) function.

a=[10,20]

print(a)

print(type(a))

b=tuple(a)

print(b)

print(type(b))

***Output***

[10, 20]

<class ‘list’>

(10, 20)

<class ‘tuple’>

In Tuples object, the index starts with 0\. The index -1 represents the index from the highest index onwards.

>>> a[0]

4

>>> a[1]

‘t’

>>> a[2]

6.5

>>> a[-1]

6.5

>>> a[3]

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

IndexError: tuple index out of range

In the above example code, the length of the tuple is 3\. So, the index range is 0 to 2\. For the index value=3, it returned an error. Tuples are immutable. So, changing any value of the Tuple object element will generate an error.

>>> a[0]=10

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object does not support item assignment

>>> a[1]=’e’

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object does not support item assignment

We can declare a tuples within another tuples; this is called as Nested Tuples.

>>> a=(2,”b”,3.2,10,(4,2))

>>> a

(2, ‘b’, 3.2, 10, (4, 2))

>>> a[0]

2

>>> a[1]

‘b’

>>> a[2]

3.2

>>> a[3]

10

>>> a[4]

(4, 2)

>>> a[4][0]

4

>>> a[4][1]

2

**6.2 Sequence Unpacking**

Sequence unpacking allows assigning multiple values at a time. The unpacking will take the elements of Tuples object and assign them to individual variables. Nested sequencing unpacking allows assigning nested sub-tuples to individual variables. For this, it uses( ).

>>> a

(2, ‘b’, 3.2, 10, (4, 2))

>>> i1,i2,i3,i4,i5=a

>>> i1

2

>>> i2

‘b’

>>> i3

3.2

>>> i4

10

>>> i5

(4, 2)

>>> (i6,i7)=i5

>>> i6

4

>>> i7

2

Sequence unpacking is also applicable to loops. In this, the range elements can be unpacked into multiple variables. For this type of unpacking, we should use tuples.

for x,y in [(10,20)]:

`print(x,y)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

10 20

If tuples are not used, then the values cannot be unpacked into multiple variables. In this case, it will generate an error.

for x,y in (10,20):

print(x,y)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “a1.py”, line 1, in <module>

for x,y in (10,20):

TypeError: ‘int’ object is not iterable

The benefits of the packing and unpacking concepts in Python programming are listed below.

■ Validates the function arguments

■ Defines the initial values of positional arguments

■ Allows to create and use different adaptors

■ Maintains log for function calls

■ Helps in the development of resilient wrappers.

During unpacking, the left side contains variables and right side contains values. The number of variables should match with the number of values provided at the left and right positions respectively. Otherwise, it is an error.

>>> (a1,a2,a3)=(200,”bns”)

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

ValueError: not enough values to unpack (expected 3, got 2)

**6.3 Methods**

An empty tuple can be created by not providing any values. A single valued tuple can be created by using “,”. If the single value is provided without “,” then it is considered as string.

>>> t1=()

>>> type(t1)

<class ‘tuple’>

>>> t1

()

>>> t1=(«a»)

>>> t1

‘a’

>>> type(t1)

<class ‘str’>

>>> t1

‘a’

>>> t1=(«a»,)

>>> type(t1)

<class ‘tuple’>

>>> t1

(‘a’,)

Multiple values can also be separated with “,”. The index of the tuple starts with 0.

>>> t1=(«a»,»b»)

>>> type(t1)

<class ‘tuple’>

>>> t1

(‘a’, ‘b’)

>>> t1[0]

‘a’

>>> t1[1]

‘b’

The ‘del’ keyword is used to delete the entire tuples. Once the tuples object is deleted, the memory allocated for it also gets de-allocated. Removing individual elements from the tuple is not allowed.

>>> t1=(“ad”,”adadfd”,”er”,23,5656)

>>> t1

(‘ad’, ‘adadfd’, ‘er’, 23, 5656)

>>> del(t1[0])

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object doesn’t support item deletion

>>> del t1

>>> t1

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

NameError: name ‘t1’ is not defined

Various basic operations of a tuple are listed below.

■ The len( ) function is used to return the length of the Tuple.

a=(30,”a”,40.67)

print(“Type of a: “,type(a))

print(“Elements of a: “,a)

print(“The length of a: “,len(a))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Type of a: <class ‘tuple’>

Elements of a: (30, ‘a’, 40.67)

The length of a: 3

■ The count( ) function is used to count the frequency occurrence of each element in the input tuple.

a=(30,”a”,40.67,30,50,30)

print(“Elements of a: “,a)

for i in a:

print(“The count of “,i,” is= “,a.count(i))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Elements of a: (30, ‘a’, 40.67, 30, 50, 30)

The count of 30 is= 3

The count of a is= 1

The count of 40.67 is= 1

The count of 30 is= 3

The count of 50 is= 1

The count of 30 is= 3

■ The ‘+’ operator is used to concatenate multiple tuples.

a=(30,”a”,40.67)

print(“Type of a: “,type(a))

print(“Elements of a: “,a)

print(“The length of a: “,len(a))

b=(15,25.3+4j)

print(“Type of b: “,type(b))

print(“Elements of b: “,b)

print(“The length of b: “,len(b))

c=a+b

print(“Type of c: “,type(c))

print(“Elements of c: “,c)

print(“The length of c: “,len(c))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Type of a: <class ‘tuple’>

Elements of a: (30, ‘a’, 40.67)

The length of a: 3

Type of b: <class ‘tuple’>

Elements of b: (15, (25.3+4j))

The length of b: 2

Type of c: <class ‘tuple’>

Elements of c: (30, ‘a’, 40.67, 15, (25.3+4j))

The length of c: 5

■ The zip() function takes two list inputs and combines them into a Tuple object.

a=[30,40,50]

b=[‘a’,’b’,’c’]

c=set(zip(a,b))

print(“Elements of a: “,a)

print(“Elements of b: “,b)

print(“Result of zip(): “,c)

■ The ‘*’ operator is used to repeat the elements of the tuples.

a=(30,”a”,40.67)

print(“Type of a: “,type(a))

print(“Elements of a: “,a)

print(“The length of a: “,len(a))

b=(15,25.3+4j)

print(“Type of b: “,type(b))

print(“Elements of b: “,b)

print(“The length of b: “,len(b))

c=(a*2)+(b*3)

print(“Type of c: “,type(c))

print(“Elements of c: “,c)

print(“The length of c: “,len(c))

***Output***

Type of a: <class ‘tuple’>

Elements of a: (30, ‘a’, 40.67)

The length of a: 3

Type of b: <class ‘tuple’>

The length of b: 2

■ The membership operator and iteration operator can be applied on the tuple.

a=(30,”a”,40.67)

#iteration operator

for z in a:

print(z)

#membership operator

if 40.67 in a:

print(“40.67 is in Tuples”)

if 100 in a:

print(“100 is in Tuples”)

else:

print(“100 is not in Tuples”)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

30

a

40.67

40.6 is in Tuples

100 is not in Tuples

■ Slicing operation is allowed for tuples. The slicing operator will select the sub-elements of the tuples. For this, we should provide the starting index, ending index and index increment values. The default value of the start index is 0; ending index is the length of the tuples. The default value of the index increment value is 1.

>>> a=(45,65,95)

>>> b=a[]

File “<stdin>”, line 1

b=a[]

^

SyntaxError: invalid syntax

>>> b=a[:]

>>> b

(45, 65, 95)

If the slicing operator is not used, then it will treat a as an array and generate an error. To overcome this problem, slicing operator “:” is used. In the above example, no values are provided for the slicing operator. So, it will copy all the values from Tuple a to b. The index value always starts with 0.

>>> a=(45,65,95)

>>> a

(45, 65, 95)

>>> c=a[1:]

>>> c

(65, 95)

In this example, the start index for slicing operator is 1\. So, the elements from Index 1 will be copied into Tuple c.

>>> a=(45,”mhj”,”nfhg”,76.34,89.45,”kjkjh”,6+7j)

>>> a

(45, ‘mhj’, ‘nfhg’, 76.34, 89.45, ‘kjkjh’, (6+7j))

>>> b=a[:5]

>>> b

(45, ‘mhj’, ‘nfhg’, 76.34, 89.45)

In this example, the ending index for slicing operator is 5\. So, the elements from Index 0 to Index 5 will be copied into Tuple c.

>>> c=a[2:5]

>>> c

(‘nfhg’, 76.34, 89.45)

In this example, the index from 2 to 5 will be used to select the elements in Tuple a.

>>> c=a[2:6:2]

>>> c

(‘nfhg’, 89.45)

In this example, the index from 2 to 6 will be used to select the elements in Tuple a. The index will be incremented by 1.

■ The == operator is used to compare the given two tuples.

>>> a=(3,4,5)

>>> b=(4,5,6)

>>> a==b

False

>>> a==a

True

>>> b==b

True

>>> b==a

False

■ The max() function will return the maximum value of the Tuple and min() will return the minimum value of the Tuple.

>>> a=(3,4,5)

>>> max(a)

5

>>> min(a)

3

■ The elements of Tuples cannot be changed. But an element of mutable object can be changed.

>>> a=(10,20,30,(40, 50))

>>> a

(10, 20, 30, (40, 50))

>>> a[0]=100

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object does not support item assignment

>>> a[3][0]=400

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘tuple’ object does not support item assignment

>>> b=(“aa”,”bb”,”cc”,[10, 20])

>>> b

(‘aa’, ‘bb’, ‘cc’, [10, 20])

>>> b[3][0]=100

>>> b

(‘aa’, ‘bb’, ‘cc’, [100, 20])

**Glossary**

■ Tuple is an immutable object and no modifications are allowed on the tuple object.

■ Sequence unpacking assigns multiples of tuple to individual variables.

■ The slicing operator allows selection of sub-elements of the sequence.

**Summary**

■ The present chapter discusses in detail about the creation of tuples and their immutable properties.

■ Deleting entire tuples is allowed.

■ Sequence unpacking is also allowed on tuple.

■ Various operations can be performed on a tuple object.

| **CHAPTER** |   | **Dictionaries** |
| **7** |   |

Python programming supports both sequence and mapping objects. The present chapter discusses about the mapping object called as “dictionary”. Dictionary is a mutable object used to maintain heterogeneous elements. It is a mutable object. Various operations are allowed on the dictionary object.

**7.1 Making a Dictionary**

Python supports various built-in data types for data structures. They are sequence or mapping objects. There are two types of sequencers. They are i) List ii) Tuple. The sequencer will access elements of the input set of elements by using an index value. The values are maintained in a sequential order. The mapping object is mainly used to access the elements without index value. Dictionary is an unordered set of elements.

Python programming supports dictionary data type. The dictionary is a mapping object. Hence, in dictionary heterogeneous values can be maintained. Each of the values is mapped to the corresponding key. The dictionary contains a set of key, value pairs. Within a dictionary, the value is accessed only with the help of corresponding key.

List is a mutable object, whereas the dictionary is an implementation of hash table and key-value pair. The list is an ordered object whereas the dictionary is an unordered object. The list elements can be any type. The elements can be accessed with the help of index value. The dictionary can also have multi-typed values. The elements can be accessed with the help of a key.

Dictionary is a mutable object. So, all the elements of the dictionary can be changed, updated, or removed. The dictionary takes O(1) look-up speed. Hence, they are found to be efficient for memory over operations and for improving look-up speed efficiency.

There are three different ways to create a dictionary. They are given below.

**i. By using literal dictionary literals “{” and “}”**

There are two literals for dictionary. They are { and }. By using these, we can create a new dictionary.

>>> d1={}

>>> type(d1)

<class ‘dict’>

>>> d1

{}

In the above example code, a new dictionary name d1 is created without any key, value pairs. So, it is called as empty dictionary. We can also provide the key, value pairs.

>>> d1={10:20}

>>> d1

{10: 20}

>>> d1[10]

20

In the above example code, a new dictionary d1 is created with a single key, value pair. The key and value are separated by using “:” operator. The left side of “:” is considered as key value and the right side of “:” is considered as value. After creating the dictionary, the value is accessed by specifying the corresponding key. For this, [] should be used.

If only value is specified without assigning a key, then it will be considered as set data type.

>>> d2={10}

>>> d2

{10}

>>> type(d2)

<class ‘set’>

The dictionary is a mutable object; so we can add multiple key, value pairs to the existing dictionary.

>>> a={}

>>> a

{}

>>> a[“one”]=1

>>> a[2]=10

>>> a[3.5]=30

>>> a

{‘one’: 1, 2: 10, 3.5: 30}

>>> a[‘one’]

1

>>> a[2]

10

>>> a[3.5]

30

Within a dictionary, multiple key-value pairs can be specified by using “,”.

>>> b={1:100,2:200,3:300}

>>> b

{1: 100, 2: 200, 3: 300}

>>> b[1]

100

>>> b[2]

200

>>> b[3]

300

>>> b[4]

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

KeyError: 4

In the above example code, the dictionary contains keys {1,2,3}. Accessing the dictionary with wrong key “4” will generate an error.

**ii. By using list of tuples**

A dictionary can also be constructed from the multiple lists of tuples as shown below. It uses the dict keyword.

>>> d=dict([(“x”,50)])

>>> type(d)

<class ‘dict’>

>>> d

{‘x’: 50}

>>> d[“x”]

50

This type of dictionary creation is useful for dynamic list of elements.

**iii. By using parallel lists**

This is similar to the previous method of dictionary creation except that, here multiple lists of tuples can be used to create the dictionary.

>>> a=(“x”,50)

>>> b=(“y”,60)

>>> c=[a,b]

>>> a

(‘x’, 50)

>>> b

(‘y’, 60)

>>> c

[(‘x’, 50), (‘y’, 60)]

>>> d=dict(c)

>>> d

{‘x’: 50, ‘y’: 60}

**7.2 Basic Operations**

■ Dictionary values can be updated by proving new value for the existing key in the existing dictionary.

>>> x={1:”a”,2:”b”}

>>> x

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’}

>>> type(x)

<class ‘dict’>

>>> x[1]=”c”

>>> x

{1: ‘c’, 2: ‘b’}

■ In a dictionary, duplicate keys are not allowed. In case of duplicate keys, only the last value will be maintained by the dictionary.

>>> q={1:100,1:200}

>>> q

{1: 200}

■ In dictionary, key can be anyone of the basic built-in data type. In addition to this, a tuple object can also be considered as key. But a list object is not allowed for key.

>>> q1={(‘key’):100}

>>> q1

{‘key’: 100}

>>> q1={[‘key’]:100}

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: unhashable type: ‘list’

■ The “==” operator is used to compare the given two dictionaries. If both are same then it will return TRUE; otherwise it will return FALSE.

>>> d={1.0:”abc”,2.0:”def”}

>>> d1={1:”abc”,2:”def”}

>>> d2={“a”:100,”b”:500.30}

>>> d1

{1: ‘abc’, 2: ‘def’}

>>> d2

{‘a’: 100, ‘b’: 500.3}

>>> d

{1.0: ‘abc’, 2.0: ‘def’}

>>> d1==d

True

>>> d1==d2

False

>>> d2==d

False

■ The str() function will convert the input dictionary to a string.

>>> d={1:”hai”}

>>> d

{1: ‘hai’}

>>> str(d)

“{1: ‘hai’}”

■ The len() function will return the length of the dictionary.

>>> d1

{1: ‘abc’, 2: ‘def’}

>>> len(d1)

2

■ The del() function is used to delete an element from the dictionary. Within a dictionary, to delete a value, the corresponding key should be provided.

>>> d1

{1: ‘abc’, 2: ‘def’}

>>> del(d1[1])

>>> d1

{2: ‘def’}

■ The del() is also used to delete the complete dictionary object.

>>> d1

{2: ‘def’}

>>> del(d1)

>>> d1

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

NameError: name ‘d1’ is not defined

**7.3 Dictionary Operations**

The dictionary contains keys and values. The details of a dictionary can be ascertained by various built-in methods of a dictionary class. They are listed below.

■ The list of keys available within a dictionary are listed by using keys() method.

>>> d1={1:”a”,2:”b”,3:”c”,4:”d”}

>>> d1

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’, 3: ‘c’, 4: ‘d’}

>>> d1.keys()

dict_keys([1, 2, 3, 4])

■ The list of values available within a dictionary are listed by using values() method.

>>> d1={1:”a”,2:”b”,3:”c”,4:”d”}

>>> d1

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’, 3: ‘c’, 4: ‘d’}

>>> d1.values()

dict_values([‘a’, ‘b’, ‘c’, ‘d’])

■ The elements in the dictionary can be cleared by using clear() method. This will create the input dictionary as an empty dictionary.

>>> d1={1:”a”,2:”b”,3:”c”,4:”d”}

>>> d1

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’, 3: ‘c’, 4: ‘d’}

>>> d1.clear()

>>> d1

{}

■ Dictionaries can be copied in using two ways, viz., shallow copy or deep copy. In deep copy, the copied object will share the same memory locations as the original object. So, changes made in one object will reflect on the other object. In shallow copy, memory will be allocated for the copied object. So, changes made in one object will not be reflected on the other object. The copy() method will perform shallow copy of one dictionary object to another object.

>>> d1={1:100.50, 2:”abc”, 3:”5+9j”}

>>> d1

{1: 100.5, 2: ‘abc’, 3: ‘5+9j’}

>>> type(d1)

<class ‘dict’>

>>> d2=d1.copy()

>>> d2

{1: 100.5, 2: ‘abc’, 3: ‘5+9j’}

>>> type(d2)

<class ‘dict’>

>>> d1[1]=986

>>> d1

{1: 986, 2: ‘abc’, 3: ‘5+9j’}

>>> d2

{1: 100.5, 2: ‘abc’, 3: ‘5+9j’}

■ The fromkeys() method is used to created a new dictionary. For the new dictionary, the sequential keys can be provided as another dictionary by the user. The user can also specify the values. If no values are provided, then none values will be initialized automatically. This method is called with “dict” class.

>>> k={1,’a’,2,’b’}

>>> d=dict.fromkeys(k)

>>> d

{1: None, 2: None, ‘a’: None, ‘b’: None}

>>> d1=dict.fromkeys(k,100)

>>> d1

{1: 100, 2: 100, ‘a’: 100, ‘b’: 100}

In the above code, the keys are defined a dictionary “k”. A new dictionary “d” is created by using dict.fromkeys() method. With this method, a new dictionary “d” is created with keys in “k” dictionary. As values are not provided, the default value is assigned to all these key pairs.

In the second case, “100” is specified as the value and the key dictionary “k” is specified for keys. So, a new dictionary “d1” is created and the value is initialized to 100 for the keys.

The list of values can be provided as an argument.

>>> v=[1]

>>> d2=dict.fromkeys(k,v)

>>> d2

{1: [1], 2: [1], ‘a’: [1], ‘b’: [1]}

>>> v=[1,2]

>>> d2=dict.fromkeys(k,v)

>>> d2

{1: [1, 2], 2: [1, 2], ‘a’: [1, 2], ‘b’: [1, 2]}

In the above code, the list of values is copied to all keys in the dictionary. To overcome this, dictionary comprehension is used.

>>>k={1,2,3}

>>> v=[‘ab’,’cd’,’ef’]

>>> d3=dict(zip(k,v))

>>> d3

{1: ‘ab’, 2: ‘cd’, 3: ‘ef’}

■ The get() is used find the input key and return the corresponding value.

>>> d3

{1: ‘ab’, 2: ‘cd’, 3: ‘ef’}

>>> d3.get()

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: get expected at least 1 arguments, got 0

>>> d3.get(1)

‘ab’

>>> d3.get(5)

>>>

>>> d3.get(5,”None”)

‘None’

In the above code, the get() without any arguments will generate an error. We should give at least one argument to the get() method. In the input dictionary, there is no key-value pair for the key=“5”. So, for get(5) call, nothing is displayed. In this case, we can provide the default value.

■ The setdefault() method is used to set initialize the key value to a default value..

>>> d1={}

>>> d1.setdefault(1,”none”)

‘none’

>>> d1

{1: ‘none’}

■ The key-value pairs in the input dictionary are listed by using items() method.

>>> d3

{1: ‘ab’, 2: ‘cd’, 3: ‘ef’}

>>> d3.items()

dict_items([(1, ‘ab’), (2, ‘cd’), (3, ‘ef’)])

■ The update()method is used to update the current dictionary object by adding a new dictionary object.

>>> d1={1:”a”,2:”b”,3:”c”}

>>> d2={100:”abc”,200:”def”}

>>> d1

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’, 3: ‘c’}

>>> d2

{100: ‘abc’, 200: ‘def’}

>>> d1.update(d2)

>>> d1

{1: ‘a’, 2: ‘b’, 3: ‘c’, 100: ‘abc’, 200: ‘def’}

>>> d2

{100: ‘abc’, 200: ‘def’}

■ The pop() method returns the element from the current dictionary object. This method takes two arguments.

 i. The first argument specifies the key to be searched.

ii. The second argument specifies the default value to be returned when the key is not found.

When the argument key is found in the dictionary, then its value will be popped from the dictionary.

>>> d1={1:10,2:”a”,3:55.43,4:”c”,5:”d”}

>>> d1

{1: 10, 2: ‘a’, 3: 55.43, 4: ‘c’, 5:’d’}

>>> d1.pop(2)

‘a’

>>> d1

{1: 10, 3: 55.43, 4: ‘c’, 5: ‘d’}

The pop() method searches the dictionary for key “2” and the value for key “2” is popped from the dictionary. If the key is not found then if the default value is not provided, keygen error will be displayed. If the default value is provided, then it will be returned.

>>> d1.pop(10)

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

KeyError: 10

>>> d1.pop(10,”no key found”)

‘no key found’

■ The popitem() method removes the topmost item from the current dictionary object. The popitem() method takes no arguments.

stack = {‘a’: 10, ‘b’: ‘str’, ‘c’: 46.3}

print(‘stack = ‘,stack)

popresult = stack.popitem()

print(‘stack = ‘,stack)

print(‘popitem() result = ‘,popresult)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms\aa>python a2.py

stack = {‘a’: 10, ‘b’: ‘str’, ‘c’: 46.3}

stack = {‘a’: 10, ‘b’: ‘str’}

popitem() result = (‘c’, 46.3)

■ Dictionary comprehension is a technique to create new dictionary with an expression. The expression consists of a pair of key and value. Within the curly braces, the expression followed by the for loop will be used to iterate the expression.

stack = {y: y*2 for y in range(10)}

print(‘stack = ‘,stack)

popresult = stack.popitem()

print(‘stack = ‘,stack)

print(‘popitem() result = ‘,popresult)

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms\aa>python a2.py

stack = {0: 0, 1: 2, 2: 4, 3: 6, 4: 8, 5: 10, 6: 12,

7: 14, 8: 16, 9: 18}

stack = {0: 0, 1: 2, 2: 4, 3: 6, 4: 8, 5: 10, 6: 12,

7: 14, 8: 16}

popitem() result = (9, 18)

**7.4 Sets**

A set is an unordered list of elements. It is a mutable object. So, changes in the set elements are allowed.

>>> s=set(“hai”)

>>> s

{‘h’, ‘a’, ‘i’}

>>> type(s)

<class ‘set’>

■ An empty set can be created by using set().

>>> s1=set()

>>> type(s1)

<class ‘set’>

■ Multiple elements can be provided in a set by using (). Within () each value can be separated by using “,”.

>>> se2=set((1,2,3,))

>>> se2

{1, 2, 3}

>>> type(se2)

<class ‘set’>

■ New elements can be added to a set by using add() method.

>>> se1.add(50)

>>> se1

{1, 2, 3, 50}

>>> se1.add((100,200))

>>> se1

{1, 2, 3, (100, 200), 50}

■ In a set, duplicate values are not allowed.

>>> s1=set((10,20,10))

>>> s1

{10, 20}

■ In a set, elements cannot be accessed by using index.

■ Adding an iterable element to a set yields error. To overcome this problem, update() method is used.

>>> s1

{10, 20}

>>> s1.add([40,50])

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: unhashable type: ‘list’

>>> s1.update([40,50])

>>> s1

{40, 10, 20, 50}

■ discard() and remove() methods are used for deleting elements from the set. If the specified element is not found then remove() will result an error.

>>> s1

{40, 10, 20, 50}

>>> s1.remove(10)

>>> s1

{40, 20, 50}

>>> s1.discard(20)

>>> s1

{40, 50}

>>> s1.remove(100)

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

KeyError: 100

>>> s1.discard(100)

>>> s1

{40, 50}

■ The clear() method is used to clear all elements in a set and makes the current set an empty set.

>>> se1

{1, 2, 3, 50}

>>> se1.clear()

>>> se1

set()

■ The pop() method will pop the element from the current set.

>>> s1

{‘a’, 40, 10, 20, ‘b’, 30}

>>> s1.pop()

‘a’

■ The “|” operator is used to perform the set union operation.

>>> s1

{40, 10, 20, ‘b’, 30}

>>> s2

{10, 300, 400, 20, 500}

>>> s3=s1|s2

>>> s3

{40, 10, 300, 400, 20, 500, ‘b’, 30}

■ The ‘intersection()’ method is used to find the set difference result.

>>> s4=s1.intersection(s2)

>>> s4

{10, 20}

■ The “-” is used for perform the set difference operation.

>>> s5=s1-s2

>>> s5

{40, ‘b’, 30}

■ The len() function returns the length of the set.

■ The all() function will return TRUE when all the elements of the set are TRUE; otherwise it returns FALSE.

■ The any() function returns TRUE if any of the set element is TRUE; otherwise it returns FALSE.

>>> s1

{40, 10, 20, ‘b’, 30}

>>> len(s1)

5

>>> all(s1)

True

>>> any(s1)

True

■ The max() and min() functions returns the maximum and minimum values in the input set.

>>> s1=set([10, 20, 30, 40])

>>> s1

{40, 10, 20, 30}

>>> max(s1)

40

>>> min(s1)

10

**7.5 Iterators and Generators**

In Python programming, an iterator is used to represent the object. With iterator, the sequence of elements is accessed with the next() method. The iter() will take iterable object and returns an iterator. If there are no next elements, then it will raise StopIteration exception.

st1=”hello”

i=iter(st1)

print(next(i))

print(next(i))

print(next(i))

print(next(i))

print(next(i))

print(next(i))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

h

e

l

l

o

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “s1.py”, line 8, in <module>

print(next(i))

StopIteration

A Generator is an iterator which controls the iteration of a loop. The generator will return a sequence of values. During iteration of a loop, the statements up to “yield” statement and the generator object with the sequence of values will be returned.

def fun():

a,b,c=1,2,3

yield a,b,c

a = a+1

b = b+2

c= c+3

yield a,b,c

a = a*2

b = b*2

c= c*2

yield a,b,c

gen1 = fun()

print(next(gen1))

print(next(gen1))

print(next(gen1))

print(next(gen1))

***Output***

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

(1, 2, 3)

(2, 4, 6)

(4, 8, 12)

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “s1.py”, line 21, in <module>

print(next(gen1))

StopIteration

v=input(“Enter the value: “)

x={k:v}

d.update(x)

for k, v in d.items():

print(‘\nKey and Value: ‘, k, v)

print(“The key is available”)

else:

print(“The key is not available”)

**Glossary**

■ Dictionary is a mutable object used to maintain heterogeneous elements.

■ Dictionary is a mapping object which maps the key and value pairs.

■ Dictionary comprehension is a technique to create new dictionary with an expression.

**Summary**

■ There are three different ways to create a dictionary object.

■ Elements in a dictionary object can be updated and deleted.

■ For each value in the dictionary, a key should be maintained.

■ The values of the dictionary object can be accessed with the associated key value.

■ Set is a subset of the dictionary.
